=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.99)
[OOPY:LAR]
HOUSTON COMMITTEE TO DEFEND AMERICA BY AIDING THE ALLIES
807-12 State Nat1onal Bank Bu1ld1ng
Houston, Texas
January 30, 1941
Honorable Tom Connally
Senate Chamber
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator:
The Houston branch of the Committee to Defend America
by Aiding the Allies at its recent meeting adopted a
resolution urging you to give your help and influence
in the stopping of shipments of gasoline and petroleum
products to Japan. Our Committee has learned from a
dependable source that approximately one hundred thou-
sand barrele of gasoline monthly 1s being shipped from
Texas ports. Practically all o this 1s going to Japan.
We are 1nformed by a leading chemist of Rice Institute
that though the grade of gasoline shipped is slightly
below that requi:ed for aviation purposes that two com-
paratively simple processes are open to the Japanese
for raising the grade when it 1s unloaded in Japan.
It 1s the belief of the Committee that we should not
place 1n the hands of an openly avowed enemy a weapon
with which to attack us. We want your help in this
particular.
We commend you for your stalwart work 1n lending ald
to England. We earnestly hope that you will give your
great skill and ability to an early passage of the
Lease-Lend Bill now pending before the Congress.
Assuring you of our good wishes and high regard, we
are
Very respectfully,
ROBERT F. PEDEN
ROLLAND BRADLEY
Chairman, Suboommittee on
Chairman
Communications

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.94)
March 26, 1941

Dear Mr. heppard:
1 learned a few days ago that iron wis still being
shipped to Japan from our country, in fact from our
port right here in Houston. 1 thought there was a ban
on shipment of iron to Japan, so am grieved to learn
we are still allowing this unchristian, also unwise
thing to be done.
How can we claim to be friends of china and help
arm her enemy?
Lr. Sheppard, every right thinking person in our
United States are distressed over this unchristian policy.
Will you please tell me why this is allowed to go
on?
Very sincerely,
Lirs. Martin Thompson

My dear Mr. Dear. Acheson:
UNT OF STATE
894.24/1260
In accordance with your suggestions this morning, I am senuing you
along first of all a list markcd Appendix A of those materials in each of
the Latin American countries which are strategic in character and which
are at present going to Japan. I have ringed those that are not of great
importance and I will try to add a slight note on some of the others. I
cannot find the copy of the Co mission's memorandum to the President of
which I thought I had a co y in my files, anu it is just as well at any
case for you to see this through Herbert Feiss, who I feel certain has one.
At any case, an inquiry to the OP for a co y, for t he information that is
contained in it, would be useful.
I am enclosing also as Appencix B, a list of instances in which ship
ments to Japan have been abnormal. Many of these concern the United States
and have now been brought under control by licensing after a very long de-
lay that was possibly inevitable. On the other hand, those that are ringed
seem to me to be worth. your attention. I have no doubt that you have this
information in one form or another through the British Purcnasing Comnission
or from other sources.
Now may I add for your information just a few of the hih spots that
it seems to me we should be seriously concerned about at once:
Mexico
APR 3
American Smelting and Refining Company in Mexico has an outstanding
commitment to the Japanese of 3, 700 tons of zinc which we badly need, and
I hope we may be able to do something to divert tnis. A delay in its de-
livery might be attributed to the opening up of the Rosita Plant, closed
down until recently by a strike tht was just settled. If the initiative
came from this end perhaps something would be done, and I will try to see
what our people will undertake.I am distressed to find that the initia-
tive for buying Mexican mercury, which somehow went off the rails, has
been allowed completely to lapse. This is important to buy, not only from
the point of view of conserving our own supplies and to avoid putting further

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
train of domestic production and prices, but also from the point of view
of preventing it getting into the wrong hands. It is one of the greatest
strategic weaknesses of the Japanese, particularly as its use for mercury
boilers seems to be tremendously increasing, perhaps in connection with
synthetic rubber. I am also distressed to learn that the 5,O0o tons a
month contracted for flurospar has been permitted to be signed by the
Japs with the companies in Mexico. This is sometring in which it seems
to me we might take a very lively interest. I am trying to stir up some
over here.
The antimony I assume is all under control since the only available
amelting is in Texas and the company is British owned. There is a leak
possible in copper since the Boleo Company is a French owned concern on
the West Coast, convenient for shipment.
I take it that I do not need to add any comment on the necessity of
plugging the Pacific backdoor, if we intend to make the blockade effective
and assist in doing so by closing Vladivostok.
I do not think that we have paid sufficient attention to mica, scrap
and graphite control in Mexico and the oil situation is notorious.
Bolivia
Bolivia is perhaps the next most important leak to close. We are
being systematically out-bid for tungsten and I believe perhaps also for
sinc, lead and antimony, though the tin is now under control and the only
thing to do is to move it to wherever the smelter is to be built in this
country. It is piling up in an alarming way in Bolivia and shipping may
not be available at the time we most need it.
The real difficulty here is the lack of effective purchasing agencies.
It seems to me that centralization in hands that can act, under policy in-
structions, swiftly and decisively and without regard to loss is necessary.
The State Department might well press this point with Will Clayton even
before any reorganization changes are made. It is impossible to buy by
present Treasury methods, as they already have been shown in the case of
Mexieo. Collaboration with the British is obviously indicated, in order
to avoid being "whip-sawed".
Brasil
The third most important source of economic leakage is Brasil from
whiah small amounts of mica and comparatively large quantities of quarts
orystals and industrial diamonds, some rutile and castor seed appear to
be going by plane over the Italian lines to Italy. This is the only
metieeable leak of which I am aware in the Atlantic, and it has been
msifically reported by the agents of the Bureau of Mines and the Ge-
doal Srtay.

=== Page 56 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Larger quantities of these same materials are presumably going to
Japan and perhaps through Vladivostok to Germany, by the Trans-Siberian.
▲ systematic purchasin6 program in Brazil would not be too expensive and
would acquire a certain number of materials which we badly need. Some
change in our own specifications to incluue all types of quartz crystas
of those now being found useful to Italy and Japan w ould be indicated.
I think this could be done by the OPi on strong enough recommendations
from the policy-forming departments of the government.
Peru
Peru forms the next most important case. We have of course bought
a good deal of copper and are buying antimony, zinc and lead, but we are
being out-bid. Some of these metals and the leakage of molybdenum,
vanadium and other products may be serious.
Chile
Our present purchasing program for copper and nitrate seems to be
adequate.
It may be that platinum in Colombia also requires control and this
could be done through New York through appropriate measures since the
companies concerned are American owmed.
The Argentine presents a small problem since the very limited amounts
of tungsten, zinc, beryllium are involved. For a small cutlay, we could
acquire practically the entire output in the East and this would be pre-
sumably useful if we embark on any such policy as has been indicated.
In all instances it would seem important to have these governments
include licensing controls, and I gather that something of that sort is
at least being contemplated by some of them. The success of any such
negotiations would depend primarily on our willingness to take practically
the entire output of strategic and critical materials as noted above.
I hope that this will serve to state the problem. It is a very scratchy
performance and could readily be superseded if the State Department in-
dicated an interest to the OPM. I must emphasize that this is a purely
personal communication on my part in answer to your inquiry, and of course
I  always happy to put any information at your disposal that we have here.
Sincerely yours,
W.y:Ello
W. Y. Elliott
P.s. Averill Harriman has told me, since this was dictated, that he sent a
sopy of this memorandum to Grady (the Memo from the NADC to the President, on
Meompmie Protection) so that it must be in your own files.

APPENDIX B
Recent evidence of abnormal shipments to Japan, either for
her own consumption or in transit to Germany or Italy, or of
direct shipment to Germany from South America.
Castor Seed from Brazil - Castor seed is useful as producing oil for
aoroplane engine lubrication. Japanese imports of the commodity are normally
from Manchuria, shipments from Brazil bein, 4,200 tons in 1938 and 5,500 tons
in 1939.
Shipments from Brazil for the first seven months of 1940 were low, but
Mitsui were in the market for 8,O00 tons at the end of July and further in-
quiries are now be ng made for 6,000 to 8,000 tons for the period of October -
December. It is noteworthy that this sudden increase in Japanese purchases
follows the entry of Italy into the wa; Italy was formerly a heavy buyer of
castor seed in Brazil and this leaas to the assumption that part at least of
these recent Japanese purchases are on Italian a ccount.
There is also evidence of purchases made through Tokio in Berlin, and
in fact, one of the principal sellers in Brazil is reported to have stated that
all those Japanese purchases were destined for Germany.
Since July about 4, 000 tons have been aispatched to Japan via New York
or West Coadt ports.
See also item 10.
Manila Hemp - Recent Japanese buyin, of this commodity has been large,
35,000 bales having been purchased in the period September - October.
3. Carbon Black - 6,500,000 1bs. have been shipped to Japan from U. S. during
the period July - September. This is equivalent to about 8 months' normal
supply. Outstanding orders are said to be exceptionally heavy, and include
one for 1,600,000 1bs. from New Ycrk to a German firm in Kobe.
4. Petroleum Coke - Exports from tne U. S. A. to Japan of this commodity are
stated to be running at a very hih level. Petroleum Coke is essential to
Japanese metallurgical and electro-chemical industries.
5. Tanning Materials - Japan normally purchases approximately 20,000 tons of
Quebracho annually in South America. During 1940 she has taken this amount up
to the end of October, but has now given additional large orders for 9,O00 tons
for shipment before the end of the year. There is no actual evidence that this
commodity is going to Germany, but there is known to be a serious deficiency
there.
Wolfram Ore - Purchase of 50 tons in Bolivia by the German partner of a
Kobe firm.

purebase of mercury in Mexico.

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
British Ambassad
MR. HORNBECK
MAR 7- 1941
DEPARTMENT OF GTATE
1.
One of the main weapons which the Democracies hold
against Japan is her dependence on them for many of the
materials without which she would be unable to maintain
her war effort.
Divi
FAR EASTE
2
The power of this weapon has already been serious-
MAR 10 199
ly weakened since Japan has been able over the last twelve
Department of State
months to import quantities of these materials far in ex-
cess of her current consumption needs. A large proportion
of these excessive imports has undoubtedly gone to build
up strategic reserves which if allowed still further to
894.24/1262
increase will shortly render Japan independent.
3.
The policy of avoiding provocation to Japan is
thus having the direct result of enabling her to make
war on us at the moment most favourable to herself. As
long as she has not sufficient reserves she is unlikely
to risk a war which must cut her off from further supplies.
4.
The first step required therefore is that a decision
in principle should immediately be taken as to whether
Japan is to be prevented from accumulating further stocks,
by restricting her future imports from controllable sources
to quantities not in excess of her estimated current con-
sumption needs. There is no need to force Japan into war
LOC
by a policy of complete economic encirclement. The denial
of excess supplies, which would enable her to build up
strategic reserves, may well suffice as a deterrent,
while in any case it will weaken her if she decides to
embark upon war.
5.
If this decision in principle can be takem, it
becomes necessary to proceed immediately with discussions
as to the means by which it can be put into practice.

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
6.
The means we have at hand are:-
(a) Fxport Licence Control
(b) Restriction of shipping facilities
(c) Preemption of key materials in Latin America
(d) Restriction of purchasing power by limitation
of imports from J pan.
7.
The co-ordination of these various methods and the
extent to which they are to be put into practice in the
various ccuntries concerned so as to avoid the risk that
the stoppage of one leak does not lead to the opening up
of another, requires detailed examination by experts.
8.
As the second step, the United States Government
might perhaps be willing to consider the establishment of
a centralized unit, representative of the several Depart-
ments concerned, and empowered to examine the above problem
and to make recommendations for the action necessary to
carry out the basic policy.
9.
In particular, it would seem of the first importance
if possible to establish such a body for the examination
of the oil problem. The danger of Japan accumulating
stocks of oil is vital since this is her most serious
deficiency and is essential to her war effort.
In view of the paramount importance of this questior
it is dealt with separately in the attached memorandum.
10.
The need for action on economic policy towards
Japan is urgent. His Majesty's Government hope therafore
that

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.93)
- 5 -
that the United States Government will be prepared without
delay,-
(a) to make the decision in principle as outlined in
paragraph 4 above,
(b) to set up the centralized unit for the purpose of
expert discussions as set out in paragraph 8 above.

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.98)
JOVISEN
MAR7-1941
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
1.
The position with regard to oil supplies to Japan
is of particular urgency; in the last six months of 1940
more than one million barrels of aviation grade spirit
went to Japan, compared with 560,000 barrels in the twelve
months of 1959. In 1941 180,000 barrels have already gone,
and orders for an additional 250,000 barrels are reported.
In Thailand stocks of aviation grade gasoline are equal
to from two to three years supply on the basis of con-
sumption prior to l940, and these stocks may well be availa-
ble for use by Japan against the Burma Road or as a reserve
for use against Malaya.
2.
We are not proposing a complete embargo; the
suggestions, which we originally submitted on November
10th, 1940 but to which we have been unable to obtain a
reply, involve co-operative action with the United States
to prevent Japan accumulating excessive stocks by curtail-
ing her use of non-Japanese tankers. 'The effect of this
would be to cut shipments by some seven and a half million
barrels per year and this, it is believed, would reduce
Japanese imports to current consumption needs.
5.
Such measures would meet the problem of quantity but
not that of quality; i.e. of the grades of oil which Japan
takes. ' This is a problem for U.S. licencing.control, for
it is only from the United States and the Netherlands
East Indies that Japan can draw the varied qualities of
oil, many of which we consider dangerous and in the cake
of the Metherlands East Indies, quantities and qualities
camporarily fimsd by an agresment with

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
The main issue is, does the United States
Government agree in principle to a policy designed to
curb imports of oil to Japan to an extent which will
prevent her accumulating further stocks?
5.
If this policy is agreed upon, does the United
States Goverrment accept our figures? If our figures are
accepted, does the United States Government agree to
our proposed methods and will they introduce supplementary
measures to cover the ground which is beyond our reach?
(See paragraph 3 above).
6.
These are matters of complicated detail for
expert examination and we regard it as an immediate
and urgent essential that we should be able to discuss
them with a centralized committee representing the
Departments in the United States Administration who
are concerned with this problem.
Detailed memoranda on the oil position in Japan
and Thailand are submitted to form a basis for such
discussions.

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
Japenese Oil Situstion
The Japanese Empire depende upon imports for 90%
petroloum needs. While it is of paramount importance to avoid 
Japan into war by restricting too drastically her supply of oil, it is
of equal importance to prevent the Japanese from accumilating further
stocks of this essential commodity, without which her Fleet and Air
Force cannot move nor her industrial effort be maintained. The Japanese
can only make an attack upon British, Dutch and American interests with
stocks of oil previously acquired from british, Dutch and American in-
terests.
2.
Borneo and the Netheriands East Indies produce more than
sufficient oil to meet the needs of the Japanese Empire and possess the
necessary refining capacity to turn this crude oil into gasoline,
lubricating oils ana otner products, as weli as facilities for the
manufacture of large quantities of aviation spirit, for the production
of which much of the N.E.I. cruae oii is especiaily suited.
3.
The oii resources of the area are controlled by the Royal
Dutch Shell and Standard Oil interests in proportions, based on crude
oil production, of approximately 70% and 30% respectively and, from
these resources, the companies serve their markets in Australia, the
Far East and other areas. Until recently Japan has only obtained a
small proportion of her imports from this source. In the year 1939,
for instance, 82% of the oil imports of the Japanese Empire were
supplied from the United States, compared with 14% frow Borneo and
the N.E.I., and 4% from other sources.
4.
In the summer of i940 an American embargo on oil for
Japan appeared not unlikeiy; Holland was no longer an effective power,
and the United Kinguom was facing the Battle for Britain. The Japanese
saw not only the urgent neeu but an apparent opportunity to extend their
control over the oil resources of the N.E.I.As a result of compli-
cated negotiations, conducted unuer strong Japanese pressure, the two
companies in November 194) entered into an agreement to increase the
supply of N.E.I. oii to Japan to an annual rate which is, in fact,
equivalent to about 39% of the imports of the Japanese Empire in 1939
from all sources. This agreement, which was negotiated with the
mowledge of the British, Dutch, and American Governments, includes
negligible quantities of aviation spirit or of crude oil suitable for
its manufacture.
5.
For many reasons the Japanese must still depend, for most
of their oil, upon the United States, where there are no restrictions
except upon certain high grade lubricants and aviation spirit of 87
octane or better. While the Japanese are obtaining relatively small
quantities of crude oil from Mexico, Peru, Saudi Arabia and Iran, and
some motor spirit and diesel oil from a subsidiary company of the
Standard Oil Company of California in Bahrein, these, with the excep-
tion of Mexico and all other practical sources of oil supply alternate
to the U.S. and N.E.I., are controlled by British, Dutch, and American
interests. Japan, in planning an attack on the N.E.I., must prepare
for an immediate stoppage of the imported supply upon which she so
largely depends.
6.
It is understood that the N.E.I. Government have plans
te damage the oil fields and destroy the refineries in the event of an
atsack. It would take many months to repair the oil wells and prebably
are than a year to rebuild the refineries, even if the necessary
Mals which, in fact, are readily available only in the United States
tained. The refining capacity of Japan itself is sufficient te

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
upom the N.E.I., the Japanese would have to face a long interim period
during which no supply of oil would be available beyond their own pro-
duction.
7.
Stocks are the critical factor. The Japanese cannot wage
a successful war without a sufficient reserve of oil to serve their Navy,
Army and Air Force anu to maintain the essential industrial effort. If
the Japanese do not already have sufficient stocks of oil to enable them
to face a war with prospects of success, it is wise to prevent reserves
from being accumulated. Restrictions, which prevent an addition to oil
stocks but are not so drastic as to force a heavy use of existing stocks,
should not in themselves incite Japan to war. The Japanese might launch
an attack for reasons unconnected with oil and with small chance of
success, or they might be altogether deterred by & shortage of oil from
undertaking the hazards of an attack on the N.E.1. If, on the other
hand, Japanese stocks are airecd, auequate, then aguin restrictions whica
merely prevent a wider margin of salety should not cause war. The
Japanese will reach & decision for reasons inconnected with their imme-
diate oil position.
8.
In November H.m.G. re-examined the Japanese oii position
in the light of estimates used in discussions, which had taken place
with the U.S. Treasury during the visit of Sir Andrew Agnew, in which
the conclusion had been reachea that Japanese stocks at the end of 1939
were of the order of 5,250,000 tons or 39,500,000 barrels.
9.
H..G. estimated totai Japanese consumption, in a war in
which the Japanese Navy was fully occupied, at not less than 7,250,000
tons (55,375,O00 barrels) per annum. On this basis present Japanese
stocks plus production from Japan, Sakhalin, Formosa, anchukuo and
synthetic sources (altogether totailing 500,000 tons per annum) would
be equivalent to about 9 months' requirements, and it was therefore
concluded that oii is the most important Japanese deficiency.
10.
The correctness, however, of the figure for stocks depends
largely on the accuracy of the U.S. Navy Department's estimates of
Japanese service consumption during the last three years. In the
opinion of H..G., judging from war experience, these estimates of con-
sumption appear too high. If this opinion is correct, it would mean:-
(a)
that Japanese stocks are somewhat above the figure
of 5,250,000 tons.
(b)
that, consumption being somewhat lower than esti-
mated, the current rate of Japanese imports would
result in a considerable further accumulation of
stocks.
11.
We were asked to inform the U.S. Government that if, on
reconsideration, the U.S. Navy were to lower their estimates of con-
sumption, H.M.G. took the view that the only reliable means of dealing
with the very undesirable situation inherent in further accumulation
of stocks by the Japanese would be by a joint policy designed to curtail
Japanese chartering of foreign flag tankers.
12.
It is estimated that in her own tankers and whalers Japan
could import at the rate of 1,800,000 tons per annum from the N.E.I.,
under the mew agreement, and a further 2,200,000 tons from the U.S.A.,
 tetal of 4,000,000 tons, or 30,000,000 barrels per annum.
At present, U.S. flag tankers are not being granted clear-
aoe fer Japam but, in addition to its own normal fleet, Japan is being
by Gertain Morwegian and other Allied tankers, as well as by
ommed by United States interests but sailing under Panamaniam

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
or other flags, and also by a few "free" Panamanian and other neutral
tankships. The monthly rate of import in non-Japanese tankers in
November was, according to H.M.G.'s estimate, about 100,000 tons.
If, therefore, Japan could no longer charter foreign tankers, her im-
ports would be reduced by an annual rate of 1,000,000 tons (7,500,000
berrels). Dependine upon her current rate of consumption, this loss
of imports kouid either prevent further stock eccumulation or would
force some withartvtl fron existine stocke.

In spite of increased dtliverite from the N.F.I., shipments of oil from

15.
Tanker controi ofiers severa. advantages. The shipping
position makes plausible, restrictions upon the use of tankers in long
voyages unessential to our own war effort or to the defense of the
Western Hemisphere. In view of the abundant supply of oils in the
U.S.A., it might be difficult for a licensing system covering all oil
products to be imposed, obviousl, airected abainst Japan, but restric-
tions on the use of tankers couid be more readily justified. The
tanker market is "tight". Moreover, our own tanker needs are urgent,
and any withdrawal of such vesseis from the Japanese trade would ease
the general position even if the ships were not put in belligerent
service. In the meantime, until the U.S. has determined its policy,
we are continuing to facilitate, with ships warrants, the movements of
tankers controlled by U.S. interests and of other neutral tankers to
Japan.
16.
The heavy increase in the oil trade for Japan has been
chiefly in oil goods carried in containers in ordinary freight vessels.
This is particularly true of high octane gasolines. Our records show
that since July nearly 1,000,000 barrels of gasoline of 86 octane, or
capable of reaching &6 octane with 3 c.c.'s of lead, have gone forward.
The President's proclamation defines aviation spirit as 87 octane or
better, and these shipments may not therefore appear statistically as
aviation spirit. Nevertheless, in practice, spirit of this quality
is a good aviation fuel. The German Air Force operates on fuel of
90 octane or less, reached only with the addition of large quantities
of lead. The U.S. has recently made the export of drums, whether
filled or unfilled, subject to license. The purpose of this may be
to curb or reduce the shipments of oil in containers. If this is so,
proposals for tanker curtailment will assume added importance as a
complementary measure.
17.
It /

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.97)
-4
17.
It is evident that economic weapons are blunted, if not
rendered quite ineffective, if a potential enemy is allowed to accumu-
late large stocks of essential commodities. These weapons cannot be
held in reserve indefinitely. Our policy is not to cut Japan off
from supplies but to co-operate with the U.S. Government in restricting,
by the least provocative means, Japanese inports of oii now going for-
wara at a rete ror which there is no comercia. justification. The
methoa which we neve proposeu is tnrougn tancer curteiinent, which would
not &ppear t an open restriction of Japene. e oii imports anu wouid, in
fact, consiuertta help our ow. uungerour shippine position.

during Sir Andrew Agnew's visit in October, and after consultation with oil
experts of Standard Vacuum and Shell Oil Company, the following conclusions
were reached.
1)
That, using the U.S. Navy's estimates for service consumption
in 1937, 1938 and 1939, supp+y hau approximatel, equalled de-
mand durin tnis three-year period.
2)

stock at the end of 1936 was approximately 39,500,000 barrels,
which the Naval Attache considered reliable, was reasonable
and probably correct.
3)
That consequently the stock at the end of 1939 could reason-
ably be assumed to be stil of the oraer of 39,500,000 barrels.

His Majesty's Government are concerned with the pos-
sibility that large stocks of oil, particularly of 86 octane
gasoline, may be accumulated in Thailand. These may be used
by the Japanese against the Burma Road or might well form a
reserve available for Japan in aggression against Malaya and
the Netierianes East Inuies.

further shipments, not perhaps large in themselves, would
serve to build stocks conveniently accessible to the Japanese.
It should be mentioned that figures recently given by the Thai
Ministry of Defense for the requirements of the country for six
months appear to be far in excess of normal. A statement of
these figures is attached.
3.
His Majesty's Government do not wish to propose an
embargo on oil for Tnailand, but they would like to see supplies
restricted to re u ar una well defined limits, and they invite
the Unitea States dovernment to give the matter their urgent
consideration.
4.
The Uniteu States uovernment is no doubt aware tnat,
owing to the dispute between the Tnai Government and the Shell
and Standard Companies, who normally serve Tnailand from the
Netherlands East Indies, the two companies have, since July
1939, shown a disinclination to supply. This has merely re-
sulted in the Thai Government turning to smaller American
companies, to supply rather than market oil, and these pur-
chases in the United States are largely paid for out of dollars
made available to the Thai Government by the Bank of England
fron Thai sterling reserves held in London. His Majesty's
Government, however, would not feel disposed to stop transac-
tions on this account except as a last resort, in which wider
issues than those of oil supply would be involved.
5.
An official of the Netherlands East Indies Government
has just visited Bangkok at the invitation of the Thai Govern-
ment in connection with their request for assistance in again
procuring oil from the Netherlands East Indies. It is under-
stood, however, that the proposals made by the Thais to govern
the marketing of products from the Netherlands East Indies are
quite unacceptable to the Shell and Standard Companies who
control these oil resources.
6.
Nevertheless, if difficulties were placed in the way
of Thailand obtaining oils in the United States the Thai Govern-
mont would very probably feel it desirable to put forward new
proposals which might be acceptable to the Shell and Standard
Companies, and the question of the re-entry of these companies
into the Thai market would therefore have to be considered in
the general question of Thailand's oil supplies. If the
United States were prepared to agree to a policy of restrict-
ing oil supplies to Thailand to regular and well defined limits
a/

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
as to quantity and quality, there is no doubt that the Nether-
lands Government and the Dutch subsidiaries of the Shell and
Standard interests would co-operate, and in this connection it
might be mentioned that a re-establishment of the marketing
positions of these companies in Thailand would tend to counter-
act Japanese economic penetration through the entry of Japanese
oil traders into the Thai market in a manner similar to Japanese
operations in China.
7.
The oil trade between the United States and Thailand
is carried on almost entirely by shipments of products in drums
and containers, and it is possible that the recently introduced
regulations subjecting such containers to export license control
will, in fact, restrict shipments of oil to Thailand. Never-
theless, it is not clear whether this will operate to prevent
shipments of 86 octane gasoline. Moreover, restrictions upon
the export of containers will not affect the export of oil if
the Thais are able either to secure tanker tonnage or to make
shipments in the deep tanks of oräinary freight vessels, and
His Majesty's Government woula be grateful for the views of the
United States Government on the questions raised above in re-
gard to the curtailment of shipments of oil to Thailand, parti-
cularly high octane gasoline, and to the possible re-entry of
the Shell and Standard subsidiaries into the Thai market from
the Netherlands East Indies.

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
Telegram from Mr. Butler, San Francisco
despatched Februery 19th.
U.S. Naval Intelligence Authorities have
reason to believe that Japanese are speeding
up all shipments from this country in anti-
cipation of major crisis during March. This
view is to some extent confirmed by fact that
"dirty" tanker "Toei Maru" due here this week
end, will be loaded by Associated Oil Company
with 71/72 octane Aviation Petrol without
cleaning her tanks. This procedure is highly
unusual. There has also been a rise in Trans-
Pacific freight rates. Similar haste in
Aviation Petrol shipments was observed in
alleged anticipation of crisis in September.
Comment by Mr. Hill:
Our information is that"Toei Maru" is due to load
86 octane petrol. A full cargo will be at least
75,000 barrels or three times Australia's require-
ments for a year.

clarations covering these two batches of cobalt ore
were secured to check on this detail.
The exnort declarations show that the cobalt ore
in uestion came trom the iiones"rine in the rovince
of Frias, Department of oto. owned by Lucheco and
Segurola. This mine is not known to the Legation and
is not mentioned among the mines covered by r. Charles
Will Wrirht of the nited  t tes Purean of Yines in his
excellent study of the " ineril Resources,  roduction
and Trade of Bolivia", published as Volume 2, No. 4 of

Department of Potosí
furnished officially to the Consulate
and it is not known in local mining circles. The exporter
on both declarations is given as the Banco Minero de
Bolivia and the destination as "New York. for the Banco
Ninero (The Grace National Bank)", ex S. S. "Imperial"
sailing from Antofagasta on December 28, 1940. The first
batch reached Antofagasta April 25, 1940, and the second
on December 5, 1940, but both were held there.
According to reliable information received by the
Legation, the shipment of cobalt ore on the S. S. "Imperial"
was not unloaded in New York but returned on the same
vessel all the way to Valparaiso, was then brought back
north by it on its next trip to Antofagasta and left
there. The Legation's information is to the effect that
it has been awaiting a Japanese ship at Antofagasta to
take it to Japan. In any event, no shipments of cobalt
ores from Antofagasta to New York or any other port
were recorded in the monthly summary of shipments from
Antofagasta received by the Legation and compiled from
ships' manifests. It would thus appear that the shipment
to New York was not manifested, which would in itself

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
be suspicious.

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
620 Culberhouse St.,
Lufkin, Texas,
March 14, 1941.
Senator Morris Sheppard
Dear Senator:
The women of all the Protestant churches are study-
1ng China, and as a special project to help China, we
want to do more than just send a little money.
Knowing that you are always on the right side of
every question we want you to know what we are thinking
and that we want to do our bit to help win this war.
We are urging you to put all your influence and pressure
on placing an embargo on oil, gasoline, cotton, and all
other war material from Texas and the United States to
Japan and other outlaw nations. We think this will help
China more than anything we could do.
We are asking you in the name of common sense to stop
this sending of war materials to outlaw nations, and to
make this really a Christian nation.
Christian women all over the world are praying for
leaders everywhere and for a permanent peace.
I speak the opinion of millions of women. Please
do all you can.
Sincerely,
The women of Lufkin, Texas, and all Christian women.
By, Mrs. L. P. Campbell, MRS. L. P. CAMPBELL
Secretary of Christian Social Relations and Local Church
Activities. First Methodist Churoh.

consummated. Whether the United Fruit

known. The Company's manager fears that

Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Secretary:
There is enclosed for your consideration, a copy
of certain paragraphs from a letter sent to me recently
by Mr. Chas. T. Austin, an attorney in Cleveland, Ohio,
who is also the vice-president of the Ohio Bronze Powder
Company of that city.
As you will observe from the enclosure, there
apparently is some difficulty, at present, in the procure-
ment of quantities of "zinc flabs" or ingot zinc. Mr.
Austin seems to feel that this scarcity is due, at least
in part, to the export of this product to Japan and other
894.24/1293
countries, and I have told him that I would make some
inquiry to see what information I could get on the matter.
It would be appreciated if you could advise me
as to the policy the government is following at present
regarding the export of zinc, and if any change in this
policy is under consideration at the present time.
Yours sincerely,
FILED

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
COPY
Paregraphs of letter received from Mr. Charles T. Austin
attorney at Law, Cleveland, Ohio
"1 am writing this letter as Vice-president f The
Ohio Bronze Powder Com, any of Cleveland.
This Company has been active in the manufacture
of bronze powders for wore than 25 years. During
the last Great War, the production of this plant
was used largely by the Governme' t for war purposes.
The plant, up to tris time, has not been called
upon by the Government to manufacture aluminum powder
as it had done during the last war.
In the manufacture of gold bronzes, zinc is a
necessary ingredient in most of these bronzes.
The Company uses what is known as "zinc Flabs"
or, in other words, ingot zinc. Scrap zinc can
be used in some in tances but not largely.
For the past year it has een growing more
difficult for The Ohio Bronze Powder Company to
purchase sufficient supplies of zinc material.
This scarcity is becoming ore and more procounced
until it has reached a point where unless relief can
be secured at an early date, this Company, and I
presume other allied companies, will be required
to discontinue their manufacture. I have read various
articles in newspapers and magazines advising that
quite large quantities of zinc hase been exported to
Japan and other countries whose interests are
antagonistic to our own. I do not believe it to
be the policy of the Government to curtail the
manufacture of American industry unless it is ab-
solutely necessary.
I am writing you this letter to bring before you the
situation which, in this particular instance, is
serious, and trust that you may have some suggestions,
or that you may take some steps whereby this stringency
may be lessened or entirely overcome.

subjeet to the requirement of an expart licemse. In

to the national defense program, applioatioms for lieease
to export zino to Japan have been disapproved.

Roferring to recent telegrams concerning Moroccan
minerals, I have the honor to report thut early in February
a Japanese governicnt mission, numbering about a dozen
persons, arrived in Casablanca. The mission included economi
9
financial end mining experts and two army officers of superior
rank, who have been commissioned by the Japanese government
U
894.
S
to arrange for the supply to Japan of large quantities of
11L
口
Moroocan manganese, cobalt, and molybdenum required by Japan'
war industries. As regarding manganese, reference is made
to the Consulate's despatch No.433 of today's date on the
NOV19
FILED
subjeot of "Morocoan Manganese" (File No.863).
Since their arrival the delegates have been much occupies
in consultations with the German armistice commissions at
Casablanca and Rabat; with the MITSUI agenoy in Casablanoa;
with M. Jean EPINAT, the direotor general of the baNIUM NORD
AFRICAIN, whioh controls the most produotive manganese and
cobalt mines in Morocoo; with the SOCIETE DES MINES DE BOU-
ARPA

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.97)
ARFA, whioh controls the manganese mines of lesser value;
and with the local representatives of the BAII UE VERNE, which
owns the only prouuetive molybdenum mine in this country.
Mr. Epinet ane is co learues t some time past have made
no secret : heir Pro-German srapetpies
er:
Low nove expressed
their willingness to export any procurable quantity of ore to
Japan but on their own terms.
Taey wish the Japanese Govern-
ment to take delivery o: the minernls frte alon side steamer
in Morocco for payment to be made in French or French Moroccan
francs to Omnium's banks in Morocco upon delivery and with the
stipulation that the Japanese government shall take care of
the ocean transport and all charges incidental thereto. The
Japanese on the other hand want to pay for the mineral only
after its delivery in Japan and demand that the ores shall be
shipped to Japan on French vessels under the French flag with
freight, insurance and all other charges at the expense of
Omnium. Under the Japanese teras there would be a considerable
delay in payments which Omnium will not accept, while the
Japanese also are endeavoring through Mitsui to have the
Protectorate Government purchase large quantities of Japanese
tea and textiles to cover Japan's disbursements in franos.
Mr.Epinat returned three weeks ago from a several days'
tour of Omnium's mines in the South, particularly the manganese
mines at Imini and the cobalt mines at Bou Azzer. He was
accompanied on this tour by Omnium's Board and by at least two
German emonomio and mining experts who inspected the mines
with him; they estimated the stooks held at the mineheads and
examined the possibilities of increasing production. They
spent most of their time at Imini and Bou Azzer and thereafter
returned to Marrakesh where they joined other members of the
Gorman

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.98)
German commission and a delegation of the Japanese. It is
not certain whether the Japanese also visited the mines, but
it anpears certain thet (1) the lerner experts are to take
control  tn romueti n  hin peou zer he also of
the molybuenum mine ut Azze our, which 1s not wneu by Omnium,
to increase and speed up prouuction; (2) the Germans, the

() the Jermans

extra rations of gasoline and charcoal. It is expected that
they also may require the Residency to assist in providing ocean
transport.
Mines and stocks.
Reliable reports inuicate that the Boards of the following
mines have eccepted orders from the Japanese mission:
SOCIETE DES MINES DE BOU ARFA (manganese) in which company
the BAN UE DE PARIS ET DES PAYS BAS, the Moroccan Railroads
and Protectorate Government are the main shareholders; this
mine lies near Bou Arfa in Southeast Morocco and is connect-
ed with Bou-Arfa and Oudjda by a normal gauge railway; and
the capacity of the deposit is estimated at about 450,000
metric tons and the content of ore at 45% metallurgical
manganese.
IMINI MANGANESE MINES which are owned by Omnium Nord Africain
and exploited through a sub-company entitled SOCIETE DE
PROSPECTION ET D'ETUDES INIERES DU MAROC or SOPEM in whioh
the Protectorate Government, as usual, is a participant;
these mines lie about half-way between Marrakesh and
Ouarzazate; the stocks held at the minehead may be as much
as 200,o00 tons of clean ore (metallurgical manganese; and
the stocks held in Marrakesh in the Omnium's depots are
about 70,ooo tons of clean ore (not sintered - all metallurgiol
manganese).
AZZEGOUR MOLYBDENUM MINE which is owned by BANQUE VERNE; 1t
Is situated 18 kilometers Southwest of Amizmis near Marrakesh;
the capacity of this deposit is undertain while the content
of the ore varies from 0.6% to 4%.
BOU-AZZER

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.98)
BOU-AZZER AND AIT AMANE COBALT MINES which are owned by
Omnium Nord Afrioain and exploited through a sub-company
entitled SOCIETE MINIERE DE BOU AZZER ET DU GRAARA Or SMAG
in whioh the Protectorate Government likewise is a participant;
these mines lie southeast of Ouarzazate between that point and
Tagora; the deposit mver hes e ully rospected and its
capacity is uncertain, but is saiu to be extensive; the stocks
at the minehead are sai excecd loo, oo. tois; stocks at
Marrakesh are reported to amount to 30,0o0 tons of clean ore;
and the content of the re is iven as :bout 12% with about 5%
nickel.
Information regarding stocks held by the Bou Arfa manganese
and Azzegour molybdenun compenies is unavoilable excent that it
has been ascertained that stocks helu ut the minehead at Bou Arfa
are reputed to be large. Vith regard to the Imini manganese, Bou
Azzer cobalt and Ait Amane cobalt mines, it may be noted that
they practically were closed down in June 1940 but were reopened
in January of this year and the work of extraction was accelerated.
At the same time, their selling agencies in Paris (the O FICE
NORD AFRICAIN which takes care of Omnium's manganese output and
the OFFICE DU COBALT, which specializes in the sale of its cobalt)
were reopened in that city under German control; both these
agencies are directed by a certain Monsieur JOUCHER, an employee of
Omnium.
Japanese orders.
Up to the present the Japanese have given orders for 30,0oo
tons of manganese from the stocks held by Omnium in Marrakesh and
for a quantity understood to be between 2,000 and 5,000 tons from
the stooks of cobalt held there by the same company. No information
is yet available as to what quantities they may be ordering from
the Bou Arfa manganese group and from the Azzegour molybdenum
group but reports indicate that they are likely to give the major
part of their orders for manganese and all orders for cobalt to
Omnium and that, in addition to the quantities of cobalt already
ordered, they may ask for 2,0oo tons more. The report that they
want 2,o00 tons of molybdenum is not confirmed.
Japanese

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
5
Japanese financial arrangements.
In principle the Japanese have agreed to pay in French franos.
In this connection, the rumors current over two months ago -
that the Protectorate wue ponsiderin importi:, Japanese reen
tea and textiles or native use - are recalled.
Mitsui in
Casablanca now has auviseu local textile importers that they
may look for large imports of Japanese cotton goods fairly
soon, while it is reported from other sources that the
Protectorate may purchase as much as 400
tons or 10,000,000
franos' worth of Japanese tea, which figures represent really
only the amount that the local tea importers' syndicate would
like. According to information received, the textile and tea
deals might be made via Indochina with the goods from Japan
to be transferred at Haiphong or at other Indochinese ports
into French bottoms upon payments to be made by the Protectorate
either directly to Japan or through Indochina. In this way,
the Japanese would recover their disbursements in francs.
Transport difficulties.
Although the question of ocean transport presents difficulties,
it is stated that an obstacle to deliveries of perhaps equal
importance is the lack of land transport; this concerns not
only the Japanese but also the Germans who are making inventories
of minehead stocks for the purpose of removing them. Omnium
cannot obtain sufficient gasoline, aloohol or charcoal rations
to transport its minerals from the mineheads to railroad
stations, even though most of its trucks have been converted
into charcoal burners, because it has been found impractioable
to use them on the steep gradients that surround the Imini
and Bou-Azzer mines whioh lie in mountain territory. Furthermore,
the monopoly of charooal distribution is held by the SHELL
COMPANY (a Frenoh Morocoan corporation in which there is a
substantial British interest), whioh, under pretert of a char-
ooal

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.95)
6
coal shortage, has reduced Omnium's rations to such a degree
that its public carrier subsidiary, the CTM (COMPAGNIE GENE-
RALE DE TRANSPORTS ET DU TOURISME AU MAROC) has had to 1ay off
about a hunared trueks.

Attitude of the Residency.
It is stated that the residency does not sympathize with
Omnium's desire to be helpful to the Axis Powers and that most
of Omnium's transport di ficulties are due to hints given by
the Residency to various authorities and to the fuel-distributing
companies. This is quite understooc by Omniun and it is
believed that it was Mr.Epinat himself who urged that the
Japanese and the Germans put pressure on the Residency to change
its attitude; he is undoubtedly the most prominent member of
the Pro-German Group living in French Morocco while it must be
emphasized that his chiefs in the Banque de Paris et des Pays
Bas and the allied SCHNEIDER-CREUZOT interests have had such a
strong influence in Morocoan affairs for many years that
frequently the Residents have been little more than their agents.
German purchases.

France. Although total figures are not available, the Consulate

Japan: Industrial
1. Several factories at Ten Nogi are employed
on govt.orders, mostly components for
government arsenals.
2. The Hirakata arsenal, where a serious fire
took place last Spring, is now making armoured
plate and guns. The damage done by the
fire has not yet been entirely repaired.
3, About 2 miles S. of Sakai, and extending for
about  2 miles in the direction of Wakayama,
the Sumitomo Company is constructing a large
industrial unit which will consist of munition
works, a power station, several factories
and barracks.
4。
A number of new munition works are being
constructed at Nishi-No-Miya.
5.
The importation of asbestos from Africa is
now valued annually at 7 million dollars.
6.
There is a snortage of shoe leather,matcies
and rubber.

no official expression of its views. He then gave me the.

and asked Mr. Hill to summarize the contents and purport

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.97)
OTL -JAPAN
With reference to the memorandum on the Japanese Oil
question which was given to Dr. Hornbeck on November 20th, and also
to Mr. Butler's conversation with Dr. Hornbeck on December l9th, a
further problem has arisen which emphasises the importance of
reaching an early decision on the proposals for restricting
Japanese oil imports through tanker control.
2.
The Japanese have now offered the Standard and
Shell Companies an increased quota in the Japanese market for
their distributing organizations. While the bulk of the oil
going to Japan is sold on an F.O.B. basis, the quantities which
are marketed by the Standard and Shell organizations in Japan,
under quota arrangements, are delivered in vessels supplied by
the companies. If, therefore, increased quotas are supplied, it
will mean that further tonnage will be made available for ad-
ditional oil imports by Japan. The Japanese are pressing fer an
answer from the companies by Jamuary 7th.
5.
The position of the companies is a diffioult one.
At one time the Standard and Shell organisations held the bulk
of the distributing busimess in Japan. In recent years, Japan
has tended to reduce the internal market quotas of these com-
894.
panies in order to build up her'own refining industry and to
conserve the marketing profits for Japanese interests. Both the
companies, supported by their respective goverments, have
through the years fought this tendeney, making constant efforto
to persuade the Japanese to increase the distribution quotas
allotted to the Shell and Standard organizations at the expense
of the quotas granted to Japan's own refineries.
This point was again pressed upon the Japanese by
the representatives of both oil compamies in Batavie during the
recent megetiatioms in the N.E.I., although it was not, of eoure
made a point of the agreement. The Japanese have now, preswsabl
beceuse of the extreme tightness of the tanker tomnage sitwatien,
offered the companies the very thing for whieh they have beem
pressimg, mmmely, an inorease in their distribution quotas, mhisk
ust be supplied in esmpany tommage.
The amount of oll to be delivered frem the .I
has been fixed by the agreesent, but it is umdersteed tiat
Stamdard Vaewm feel that, as a commereial matter, theg hare

=== Page 231 === (confidence 0.98)
If the sompanies aésept and supply à
quotas, the affest will be to imarease the tommage
is of importance, not only because of the resultimg imuress
in Japan's intake of oil, but also because the tonmage will have
to be removed from other service and supplied for the Japanese
trade at a minimum of cost to the Japanese at a time when tsnker
tonnage everywhere is scarce and when H.M.G. are having difficulty
in meeting their own requirements.
7.
Another point raised by this proposal is the
effect that such supplies may have upon the Japanese trade in
China. It is well known that the Japanese have been pursuing
aggressive and ruthlessly discriminatory tactics 'in an effort to
dominate the oil trade in China and to drive out the established
concerns. The limiting factor on this has been the difficulty
which the Japanese marketing concerns in China encounter in
obtaining supplies and facilities for distribution.
8.
The Shell and Standard Companies have been doing
their utmost to prevent this domination of the Chinese market by
the Japanese. They are further endeavouring at this moment to
make:an arrangement with the California Texas Oil Co., the third
of the large established concerns in the Chinese market, which
will bring thet Company's policy more into line with their own
and prevent the Caltex from supplying the Japanese "re-sellers"
in China. It is felt that additional quota supplies of products
by Shell and Standard to Japan will release a further supply of
oil products from Japan into China, and will therefore have an
effect exactly contrary to the objects of Standard and Shell
policy in China, where, it is hoped, their policy will shortly
have added force from the cooperation of CalTex, if this is
secured.
As matters and the regulations of the countries
concerned now stand, the amount of oil available to Japan by
import is limited to the extent that Japan finds difficulty in
obtaining the tonnage required to carry it.
10.
The extreme tightness of the tanker tonnage sitwm-
tion the world over, which has been increased by the recent
requisitionings by the U.S. Mavy Department and the fact that
the Japamese are demanding a decision by the Standard and Shell
Companies on the question of increased quotas by Jamary 7th,
gives, in the view ef I.M.G., additiomal urgency to the questiem
of our reaching a desisiom em the gemeral prepesals for tanker
curtailment suggested in the memorandum of November zOth. L.M.G.
are therefore anxious to know if the State Departmemt are in
a position to agree to the proposals then put fosward.

March
Mr. A. F. Campbell
Washington Office
Dear Mr. Campbell:
Several weeks ago we wrote the State Department to inquire whether bearing
shipments to Japan and Russia were regarded unfavorably by the Administration
Ten days later, receiving no reply, Mr. Umstattd renewed the inquiry informing
the War and Navy Departments as well as the Department of State that heavy
new inquiries for machine tool bearings for Japan had come in. Replies,
copies of which are enclosed, were received from the Navy Department and
the Army and Navy Munition Board. Neither of these gave a definite answer.
Meanwhile, the management decided to refuse to quote on about $75,Ooo worth
of machine tool bearing inquiries from Japan, and in addition have canceled
Japanese orders amounting to $250,000 and Russian orders of $26,984. We also
imformed Muller, Phipps, and Sellers, our exporting agents, that we would accept
no future orders from either Russia or Japan under present conditions. This
decision was also communicated to our New York and San Francisco branches.
While we have no doubt of our ability to fulfill our domestic customers'
demands, we do not desire to sell to Japan or Russia because such saies would
mean indirect exportation of nickel and would also help in building up the
Japanese and possibly the German munitions industry.
While the Germans are probably receiving great quantities of bearings from
the S. K. F. Company plants in Germany and Sweden, the mechanized character
of their armaments demands every bearing which they can lay their hands on.
We have no desire to assist either Germany or Japan or Russia and have de-
cided not to sell bearings to any of them.
In order that we may be on record, I enclose copies of this letter whih
ILHD
would appreciate your delivering personally to the State Department, to
Lieutenant Commander Harrison and to Lieutenant Colonel MacMorland.
2
Sincerely yours,
PS/BAS

WRobert Jimhen

Department n!
rme
My dear Mr. Welles;
I am writing to you as I have to other
members of the State Department and members of the United
State's Senate concerning the continuation of the arms
traffic with Japan.
As a Republican who has fully supported
the Foren Policy of the Administration for the past six
years and as one who has worked and argued for the passage
of the Selective Service Act and the Lease-Lend Bill,so
called,I find myself aa compete loss to understand the
attitude of the Administration and the State Department in
their tacit allowance of the sinews of war to be shipped to
Japan.This is a policy that has been doubly difficult for
me to become reconciled with since the Three Power Pact and
since the Speech of President Roosevelt last week in which
our active participation in the world conflict was unequiv-
ocally announced.
As one who comes within the agelimits of
the Selective Service Act and as one who comes into elose
contact with members of the armed forces of our nation,it
is my observation and opinion that this logically inconsist-
ent attitude and policy of the United States 1s one which
has the whole hearted condemnation of the great part of
our thinking populace. My frienas in the Army and Navy and
my acquaintances within the draft age limits agree that It
makes little sense to them to be aked to fight againsta
power such as Japan with whom this nation has been steadily
supplying with cotton,scrap iron and fabricated metals,1
89
and gasoline,aircraft,and cotton.I have letters on my doek
at this moment from Senators Tobey,White,and Bridges in which
they voice their condemnation of this policy and in which
they state that they will do all within their power to stop
this nefarious international trade.
No doubt,Mr. Welles, you are of the same op-
1nion, and if you are, may the people of this nation rely on you
to use your vast influence with the President and with Mr.Hull
to embargo such traffic to Japan.If we are 1n this war as we
are,let us be sincere 1n our efforts to aid China and Britain
and not play both ends against the middle and the people of
our country for suckers.
Respectfully yours,
Hemry m. Fuller

My gear r. Secretary:
Fealizing the grave responsibilities of your office at this
6
time it is with hesitation that I add anything. But I feel that
I must get clarified if possible the situation referred to herein.
At a recent luncheon of a large number of businessmen in
North Carolina it was reported that our nation is now selling large
quantities of gasoline to Japan. This I understand to be a fact,
but I was not well informed enough to state the policy upon which
it is based. But more important, it was charged and contended by
a prominent man who is connected in a substantial way with the
petroleum industry that much of the gasoline being sent to Japan
is finding its way through Russia to Germany. This I contradicted
as being illogical and unjustifiable. Upon both points I need any
available information.
This would not be troubled with at all but for the rather
FILED
it was maintained.
With my best wishes, I am

SUBJECT

894.24/1320
From the figures available here on the Japanese asbestos trade, it
appears that they import quite a bit of crude and do most of their own
manufacturing. The data do not, however, indicate whether the material
is critical in Japan or not with sufficient clearness to warrant recom-
mendations. They have no raw material of their own and import most of
their crudes from Canada. I suggest that our foreign representatives
be requested to send by cable information as to whether there is any em-
barrassment in Japan in securing requisite supplies and whether, if
possible, they have any extensive stock pile.

FE states they have no information as
to supply of asbestos in Japan.
Japanese legislatlon makes it diffi-
cult for our Embassy to obtain information
in this regard but a cable might well be
sent asking for any information obtainable.
The Canadian Chargé d'Affaires states
that in 194l Canada establ1shed a quota
on the export of asbestos to Japan of
fifty percent of the previous five-year
period. The quota 1s subject to the re-
quirement of export licenses and no
lieenses are given for no. 1 and no. 2
asbestos.
It appears that the quota might have
been muoh smaller were it not for the fact
that exports of this commodity were going
to Japan from the United States. Some con-
fusion in this regard has been caused by
lack of identical definition in Canada and
in the United States of various classes of
asbestos.
The Chargé d'Affaires 1s sending me
a note today on the subject.

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.98)
Mrs. Samuel Gilmore Dickinson
749 Portland Avemue,
St. Paul, Minnesota
Apr1l 9, 1941
Senator Henrik Shipstead,
Washington, D. C.
My dear Senator:
I ardently wish that the sending of scrap iron,
oil and other war materials to Japan be stopped firmly
and immediately. It 1s my understanding that we are
still supplying Japan with war materials. It seems
to me a foolish policy to arm your enemy. I furthermore
believe that a firm stand taken by us now might force
Japan to desist from further expansion. I would rather
fight a small war now, than a big one later. I think
the U s. position now is comparable to that of France
and England when Hitler militarized the Rhine! Let us
stop Japan now when it would be comparatively easy,
rather than Tater when it will be harder with a con-
quered China at her back and perhaps a beaten British
Empire.
Sincerely,
Edwine Dickinson
(Mre. S. G.)

UF STATE
With further reference to  y telegram no. 10
February 11, 3 p. m., I lave the honor to report that
the American ship S.S. Scottsburg arrived at this
4r.
port on February 20, 1941, carrying for the account
of Mitsubishi Shoji Kaisha a cargo of 37,904 drums
of gasoline each containing about 53 gallons and
834 tons of diesel fuel oil in bulk amounting to
PS/LB
5,874 barrels.
Total arrivals of gasoline at Tsingtao from
America on American ships for Mitsubishi Shoji
Kaisha during February amounted to 69,988 drums or
MAY 1 9 1941
FILED
approximately 3,709,364 gallons. It is estimated
that this gasoline total is more than all the oil
companies including British and Japanese sold in
this area during 1940.
The 834 tons of diesel fuel oil imported repre-
sents a small amount in comparison to total imports
during...

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
during 1940. Last year Japanese interests imported
13.241 tons of diesel fuel cil corpared to 3,941
tons.
twe eit sterore tarbs with  cupueity
of ?,ooe tons eacl were emg loted for : itsubishi
Shoji Kaisha last year and have been useu to store
the recently arrived oil.
 fr s eum be seertnined neither the prusoline
nor diesel fuel oil were assessed duty by the Chinese
Customs and it is therefore presumed that the shipments
were destined for military and naval use. Very little
diesel oil is sold commercially at Tsingtao. Another
fact pointing to the military character of the ship-
ments was that the American ships were immediately
given a berth at No. 3 Wharf upon arrival. No. 3
Wharf is not open to Third Power vessels and when
not occupied by Japanese military transports, it is
made available for Japanese ships only on a restrict-
ed basis, i.e. when all other berths at the No. 1
and the No. 2 Wharf are in use.
The cargo from the American ships, S.S. Aquarius
and S.s. Scottsburg, was discharged rapidly and with-
out incident.
The officers and crew of both ships
except the Captain remained aboard while the vessels
were in harbor. No attempt was made to secure shore
passes for the officers and men from the Japanese
naval authorities here since the Captain and the
ships' agent desired to avoid the possibility of
any incidents ashore.
The...

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.96)
-3 -
The Consulate concurred in this position, but
t wuao cloar to the Captain of both ships that
made te rr n e for the
me ashore. It is
understood that there was some agitation among
the men on the Scottsburg for shore liberty, but
thut whe the went ef the steonship company endeavored
to cet permission for the men to come ashore, the
Japanese military authorities would grant only such
limited liberty that the men decided to remain aboard
ship. The agent was inforned that the Japanese
authorities desired that application for passes be
made by the agent of the steamship company rather
than by this Consulate.
In this connection I wish to state that the ques-
tion of shore liberty in the case of these two ships
was complicated by the fact that the ships were tied
up to a military dock while unloading gasoline and to
a military-controlled installation for the discharge
of diesel oil, and if shore liberty had been granted
there would, without a doubt, have been numerous
incidents with military sentries.
Respectfully yours,
Papw. huyp.
Paul W. Meyerl

April 14th, 1941
The Honorable James M. Mead,
nashinyton, . C.
My dear Senator;
I am very much puzzled by two items I read in the
newspepers today,
1.
100 cars containing fet are now on their way
from Chicago to the Pacific Goast to be shipped
to the Far East,
2.
The Germans have taken from Italy all the olive
oil they can and shipped it to Germany to feed the
German people and to lubricate the machinery.
Our government must be well awere that shipments such as
this one I have noted ere ultimately destined for Germany
and I am asking you to tell me why our representatives
are not stop ing this traffic.
what 1s the advantage of depriving the Axis powers of
goods they want and so helping Pritain in the Atlantic,
and supplying these same powers with what they want via
the Pacific?
I shall await your kind reply with the greatest interest.
Respectfully yours,
MADELEINE C. CURTIS

In view of Mr. Berle's memoranda of February 7
and February 13, 1941, a brief eummary of the Universal
Oil Products Company-Japan Gasoline Company case may be
of 1nterest. On June 1l, 1940 the Japan Gasoline Com-
33
pany brought a 10,000,000 suit agsinst the Universal
894.
Oil Proiucts Compeny for the latter's failure to perform
1ts obligat1ons under the July 19, 1928 and August 19,
1938 contracts between the two companies. Both contracts
0
called for the furnishing by the Universal Oil Products
C
FILED
P
Company of technical information such as plans and speci-
fications for the production of gasoline. The 1928 contr ot
related to processes for the production of motor grade
gasoline whereas the 1938 contract related to processes
for the manufacture of aviation gasoline. After the an-
nouncement on December 20, 1939 of the "moral embargo" on
the furnishing of information required for the production
of aviation motor fuel to certain countries, the Universal
10E1
011

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
O1l Products Company stopped performance on both of these
contracts.
After considerable iscussion between officials
of both companies and officers of the Depertment, it was
agreed that the Department had no objection to the
Universal Oil Products Company resuming performance
under the 1928 contract between the two companies.
This decision was reached beceuse it was determined that
the information to be furnished under this 1928 contract
related only to the making of motor gasoline and there-
fore the furnishing of this informetion did not come
within the purview of the "moral embargo" relating to
the dissemination of information for the production of
aviation gasoline. Pursuant to this action, an agree-
ment was signed by attorneys representing both companies
to remove from the realm of the litigation any question
of breach of the 1928 contract.
On September 7, 1940 the Japan Gasoline Company
served an amended complaint on the Universal O1l
Products Company whereby no remedy was sought under the
1928 contract but specific performance of the 1938 con-
tract was demanded w1th $2,000,000 damages for delay
or, 1n the alternative, $10,000,000 damages.
A 11cense was 1ssued on November 2, 1940 to the
Universal Oil Products Company for the exportation to
Japan

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
Japan of designs and specifications for the construc-
tion of a gasoline plant. thia license covering the
technicel information omin unier the looe  ntract.
Two applicstione of the Universal Cil Products Company
for license to export to Japan technical information
coming under the 1938 contract were rejected on Novem-
ber 6, 1940.
A memorandum left with Mr. Berle by Mr. Morito
Moriahima, Counselor of the Japanese Embassy, on the
latter's visit on October 23, 1940, expressed thanks to
the Department for the use of its offices, which made
possible the fulfillment on the part of the Univereal
O1l Products Company of the terms of the 1928 contract.
Reference was made, however, to the damage suit filed by
the Japan Gascline Company against Universal for
non-fulfillment of the terms of the 1938 contract. It
was pointed out that an amicable settlement outside of
court was desirable and that the Embassy would recommerd
to the Japan Gasoline Company that it withdraw its
suit, for the time being at least, if the Department
would grant licenses for the exportation of all the
"processes" 1n the 1938 contract "which do not relate
immediately to the production of high quality aviation
gasoline".

JULIAN RANDDLPH

February 4, 1941
Mr. Roger S. reene, Associa: e . irector
