Dear Mr. Greene:
I was indeed pleasod to receive your letter of January l4, and
especially interested to kmow th t you are investi ating the matter
of export licenses to see how this new authority 1s bein used. In
my opinion, the need is not to and new conmodities to the list of
those requiring export licenses, but rather (with a few exceptions)
to establish a firmer export-restriction policy in regard to the
commodities now already under license.
From the last paragraph of your letter I gather that you would wel-
come my opinion as to whether the subject of embargoes should at
this time take second place to the aatter of imnediate aid for England.
It is my firm belief that both marters are "firsts." To assure our
om survival we must , Ive all possiule aid to n land, immediately;
but likewise we must imnedlately take a stron stand in the Oriental
situation before it ets out o! our control.
Unless we take imnodiate posltive action, Japan and the United States
are now headed inevitably toward ar. The stakes of that war are the
rubber, tin, and oil of southeastern Asia (including Netherlands
Indies) and possibly the iron ore supplies of our own Philippines.
Japan aims to bring these raw materials under her own domination,
while the United States, being greatly dependent on these materials,
will certainly not allow such a domination of them by Japan. The
political pressure of our own industrialists, faced with destruction
through loss of raw materials essential to their existence, would
force the United States to challenge Japan militarily on such an
issue. Even if appeasement were considered, the United States would
have only two alternate choices, (1) to acquiesce and thereby be
deprived of the materials which are indispensible to our own osonomy
and our aid to Britain, or (2) to defend our right of access to these
materials by means of military and nayal conflict with Japan, there-
by imobolizing our fleet in the Pacific so that we could not defend
our eastern seacoast or aid England in the Atlantic. In either case,
of course, our own defense against Germany would be dangerously re-
tarded.
I am convinced that the above developments are the inevitable result
so long as we pursue an appeasement policy with Japan. Furthermore,
they lead inevitably to a dilemma in which, whatever course of action
we choose, we will have lost the opportunity to give adequate aid to
ingland.

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.98)
 Roger S. Greene, Associate Director
Cemmittee to Defend America by Aiding the Allies- February 4, 194l
-2-
There is, I belleve, mly on solnt  w i" is he least lixely
to lead to war. In brlef  is or he uni od states and ritain
to
(1)
Avoid embart oes of four co modity  roups hich Japan
can obtain in an important amount In Asia, Nether-
lands Indies, or the Philippines. These are petroleum
products, iron ore, rubber, tin. (Netherlands Inuies
also should avoid such embargoes.)
(2)
Immediately place an air tight embar o on all other
war materials, and maintain that embargo rolentlesaly
until the danger of a Japanese-American war has been
dofinitely eliminated.
Admittedly, petroleum, iron ore, rubber and tin are valuable to a
nation at war. On the other hand, they are valuable only insofar
as they are component parts of a complete economic structure, and
are useless to a nation deprived of many other commodities by which
it maintains 1ts industry and carries on its conquest.
In defense against United States' power in regard to embargoes,
Japan has many times threatened retaliation, even as early as July
1939 when the United States decided to abrogate the Japanese-United
States Trade Treaty of l9ll. do ever, Japan's failure to attempt
such retaliation at any time to uate su ests that the threats are
moroly bluff. In fear of bein cut oif from Alerican war materials,
Japan has decided on a threatenin, oluffin, policy instead of
actual retaliation.
Japan knows that 55.7% of her war materials come from the United
States (and 8.7% from Canada, makin a total of almost two-third
of Japan's foreign supply) and that most of these materials can
not now be obtained in appreciable quantity from any other source.
There is just one exception to this Japanese attitude of combined
bluff and fear. This exception was well expressed on October 2,
1940 by the Japanese newspaper Asahi, as follows:
"If Britain and America ban oil, rubber and tin exports
to Japan, we would choose an ail or nothing struggle
rather than waiting for death."
(To Asahi's list, I would add iron-ore because of our
large supply of that commodity in the Philippine
Islands.)

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.98)
Roger S. Greene, Associate Director
ittee to Defend America by Aiding the Allies- February 4. 1941
-3-
You will recall hat I covered many other aspects of t Is roblem
in my treat1se of October , 1940, " o.. omic Sanctlons and the War,
A Flan to Lefend America y raralyzin the Japanese  ar Mac ine.
a copy of which you ha e. In that recomrendation, written over
three months ago, 1 su, ested a gradual applicat on of embargoes.
However, the time for gradual application has passed. Because of
the increased acutoness of the situation, our action must now be
quick and decisive. As some one has said, it is "later than we
think."
In the above mentioned analysis, " sconomic sanctions mnd the ar,"
you will note that the ultimate  oal of my plan is to brin Japan
into our own orbit as a friendly cooperator in a peaceful Pacific
Basin. This can be done, not through appeasement but through a
vigorous embargo program which will make Japan realise that the
United States and Japan must peacefully cooperate.
The Japanese  overnment spokeamen have officially made it clear
that ail they need is time and freedom from American interference
to establish the "New Order in Greater East Asia" and, with Germany
and Italy, to establish a joint domination of the globe. At suoh a
time the United States wlli ha e lost the power it now has to con-
trol the peace in the Pacific Basin.
With the Netherlands Indles, tiny aolland was able to become the
world's third greatest commercial power. ith these sane Nether-
lands Indles, plus China, Into-China and possibly Thailand, Japan
will be able to build an Oriental enpire which can wrest the con-
trol of the Pacific from our hands, commercially and militarily.
If that occurs we must look forward to an era, possibly of mony
decades, when we will not have peace in the Pacific but, rather,
a continuous succession of wars and threats of wars.
Possibly in only one way can the United States avert such a catas-
trophe. We must assume immediate control of the situation by means
of an immediate and relentless embargo of our exports of war mater-
ials to Japan. Without such materials, the whole Japanese program
of conquest is certain to collapse, for most of her war" is being
waged with our material. If such an embargo is handled seleetively
and strategleally as desoribed above, the chances are lo0 to 1 that
military or maval confliot can be avoided.
A few weeks from now, however, the situation may be entirely
different. When Hitler's invasion of England is again attempted
in the Spring, we may have dire need for use of our fleet in the
Atlantie - and then, almost certainly, Japan will start a program
of aniping, or of major conquest, which we are powerless to prevent.

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.98)
ger 8. Greene, Associate Director
Attee to Defend America by Aiding the Allies- February 4, 1941
-4-
We will at that time robably ve forced into war with Japan
(unless we he e  revented it throu ember oes); ane this will
greatly curtail our aoility to help a. l d in  he ost critical
period in her entire history.
A good definition or appeasement is "to avoiu minor issues until
they have grown into major uncontrollaule crises." There is rave
danger that this is just what wo aro or doin. in the ca e of Japan.
In this eleventh hour, e  lll ha e o ortunity, throu h vigorous
embar oes, to prevont a war itt J a, or, equally i portant, to
prevent those snlpin operations and threats which most sirelj will
nullify our e rorts to ive all aid to n. land.
But we must act quickly. "It is later than we think."
President Roosevelt and Secretary Hull have cleared the way for
action by placin many commodity groups under the export license
system. No more proclamations aro needed. All we need now 1s to
ostablish a rigiu policy of refusing export licenses to Japan.
While it is difficult to obtain information on actual license ro-
fusals, the general impression is that our government is refusing
so few licenses that the licensing systel is merely a form and our
embargo program inelfective.
The strategy of avoiding war demands, however, that the United States,
Britain and Netherlands Indies immediately assure Japan that we shall
not refuse licenses on the four "danger conmodities," p troleum, iron
ore, rubber and tin unless Japan invades or threatens our possessions
or th Netherlands Indies.
In the matter of our sonding a naval squadron to Asiatic waters, I
believe this is desirable whether we increase the scope of our em-
bargoes or not, but it is more necessary in case we do not extend
the embargoes than if we do. If  e do not imnediately take vigorous
control of the situatlon throuh extensive embargoes, then we have
no means of defending our indispensible sources of rubber and tin
except through naval action. Today we can easily spare a portion
of our fleet for Asiatic waters because we have no immediate demand
for it in the Atlantic. In the near future, however, we may not be
so favorably situated; we mi ht be forced to cope simultaneously with
naval conflicts in both Pacific and Atlantic. That is the main argu-
ment for tightening the embargoes today, to bring the Oriental situa-
tion under control before it ls too late.
It is because of this matter of naval aotion that I propose except-
ing from the embargo, the four "danger" commodities, petroleum
produets, iron ore, rubber and tin. For, with these four commodities
definitely exeepted from the embargo program, Japan would be in a

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.98)
Reger S. Greene, Assooiate Director
mittee to Defend America by Aiding the Allies- February 4, 1941
-5-
position where she ad nothin
aie une ov r t in to lose by
attacking the Philipplnea or etherlands indies. in such a situa-
tion, our Asiatic fleet (which sho l be statloned only at Sin apore
and south of the Equetor) would be needed merely as an added threat
to prevent a war which, at worst, would be unlikely.
Unquestionably, our own safety demands that we immediately lay plans
for increasin, our aid to Britain. But, wh le doing this, we must
not fail to take immediate control o a situation in the Orient which,
without such control, would make our aid to England either ineffective
or impossible.
We are now supplying Japan with materials which are definitely needed
to speed up our aid to England. With a limitation on our own produo-
tion facilities which during l94l will amount to a major crisis and
possibly cause the fall of England, we cannot continue both to supply
England and those forces which have avowed to wreak her destruotion
"if only given time."
So in answering your letter I would say that I believe the Japanese
embargo program and that of giving all possible aid to England should
both be stamped by our government with the words "1munediate action."
At about the time this letter reaches you, Aamiral Nomura, will have
Just arrived in Washington to take up his new work as Japanese Ambas-
sador. A friendly person, well known for his desire to cooperate
with the United States, Admiral Nomura may personally aspire to the
same ultimate goal which I recommend, namely a peaceful Pacific Basin
in which the United States and Japan will both benefit by the mater-
1al advantages of unrestricted trade and free access to needed raw
materials.
Unfortunately, however, Admiral Nomura's actions cannot be controlled
by his own personal aspirations but by the orders which he receives
from the military clique which now rules Japan, dominates the Orient
and defies the United States. In our own defense, we must not allow
ourselves to be lulled into inactivity by his pleasing phrases, but
should, today, take vigorous embargo action to assure Japan that we
demend respest for the United States. Having first acccmplished this
Indispensible purpose, we can, I am sure, count on Admiral Nomura and
Seeretary Hull to rebuild a new Japanese-American relationship which
will be satisfactory to both nations.

American Aviation Gasoline to be Bootlezged to the Japanese Navy
Copies of a confidential letter and contracts written by the
Ulriah Fuel Company, a German firm in Shanghai, have been received.
These docuzents outline a plan whereby the Ulrich Fuel Company will
obtain aviation gasoline from the United tates for the use of the
Japanese Navy through an American firm, the North American syndicats
According to the letter, ir. R.. Boyd, of the North Amerieam
Syndicate, is supposedly very pro-Chinese. He has in the past suce
in importing goods into China and turning over such goods to the
Chungking government. He therefore enjoys the confidenee of Chungid
The scheme is that the North American Syndicate should purehase avi
gasoline from the United States. The Japanese will permit this gaso
to enter at the ports of Ningpo and Wenchow. The Japanese dll, furt
more, see to it that about 20 per cent of this gasoline gets through
their lines and is actually delivered to Chungiing Governmsnt repre
tatives. This is absolutely necessary in order to keep Mr. R3. Bayd
in the good graces of Chungicing. The other 80 per cent of the gasol
wdll be confiscated by the Japanese. The Ulrich Company acts a 
between between the American Company and the Japanese. The Japanese
and the Chinese will get aviation gasoline (the Japanese will get fd
fifths of it), and both the Ulrich Company and the North Ameriean Sy
dicate will get fat ocommissdons, and Mr. Boyd will remain in the god
graces of Chungking and the U.S.A., according to this plan. Mr. Bagd
is due in Washington soon to make arrangements. A draft of a contred
whereby the Japanese Navy undertakes to purchase by the above means
tons aviation gasoline, 2,000 tons aviation lubricating oils, and 
tons Diesel oil was sent by the Japanese Naval Attache, China, to Te
on Jamuary 2, 1941. The Japanese Navy used a puppet firm, the Chake
Kaisha Limited. The actual contract is drawm up between the Ulrick P
Company and the Chuka Koku Kaisha.
Comment:
None of the firms mentioned are listed in the China Heng
List of 1940 and nothing 1s knowm of R.S. Boyd.

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.98)
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
March 8, 1941.
I am in agreement that this Gov-rnment
should take steps directed toward restricting
exports to Japan to normal cuartities.
I am not convinced in my own mind as to
what would be the best procedure for this
Government to follow toward carrying out such
an objective. I am inclined to the view that
it would be preferable to set un no new
organization or committee but to roceed as
we now are: simoly exohanging information
fully with the British Government and then
deciding through agencies and procedures
already in effect what action, if any, this
Government should take. I would be inclined
to make an exception with regard to petroleum
products, because the problem here is so many-
sided and difficult, and to favor the setting
up of an interdepartmental committee to study
the whole question of petroleum products.
It seems to me that the question of re-
stricting exports to Japan or of restricting
imports from Japan is essentially a question
of foreign relations. I am therefore dubious
as to the advisability of having this subject
turned over to a committee on which the pre-
ponderance of numerical representation would
rest with representatives of other departments.
3.2/11

(5) Chemicals and Related Products (except certain products, such
as pharmaceutical preparations, etc., to be specified after furth
study; to be used for pharmaceutical purposes in Japan). Foreign
5
Funds Control will deny.
C. Petroleum Products and tetraethyl lead. Foreign Funds
Control will deny except where an export license has been issued.U
D. Cotton. There are pending before the Foreign Funds
m
Control 10 applications covering the export of 27,500 bales of
cotton to Japan, the value of which is approximately $2,000,000.
It is stated in the letter addressed by the Under Secretary of
State to the President under date of July 31, 194l, that raw
cotton exports to Japan have averaged about $600,000 per month
in the first six months of l94l, as against slightly over
$4,000,000 per month average in 1938. A memorandum is being
prepared by the Treasury Department discussing exports of cotton
to Japan and making recommendations with respect to the licensint
thereof. No action is being taken by the Foreign Funds Control
a decision is reached as to what quantities of cotton are tobe
licensed, on a monthly or other basis, for export to Japan.

3. Attention is directed to the fact that the Foreign Funds
Control is holding without action a group of applications involving
exports from Latin America to Japan, to be financed by means of
debits to blocked Japanese accounts in the United States. A list
of some of the pending applications has been furnished to the
State Department. No action will be taken on cases in this
category pending a policy determination.

August 20, 1941
IRON ORE EXPORTS FROM THE PHILIPPINES TC JAPAN
Our quote for the export of 1ron ore from the
Philippines to Japan was fixed at 525, 000 tons, for
the last seven months of the year, this quota being
based on the 1938 annual export of 900, 000 tons.
Since 620, 000 tons were exported during the first five
months of 194l, total exports during the year, if the
894.24/1
entire quota 1s permitted to be exported, will be
1,145,000 tons or only very sl1ghtly less than exports
during 1940. On the other hand, of this amount
894.24
852,000 tons had been exported by July 31, leaving
3
less than 300, 000 for the remaining five months of the
6
year.
14
Exports of iron ore from Malaya to Japan were
5
approximately 1,600,000 tons in 1938 and rose to
approx1mately 2,000,000 tons 1n 1939-40, to wh1ch
P
latter figure they have until recently been limited.
MAY 1 3 1941
S
FILED
ET
The British estimate that by the recent prohibition
of night loading they have caused a forty peroent
reduction in the rate of export, though this estimate
would appear to be a rather uncertain one.
If
11

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
If the British estimate 1s correct, their exports
during the rema1nder of the year w1ll be about 500, 000
tone or about one~third of their total 1938 exports.
American ahipments during tht remainder of the year,
if the quota 1s not changed, will also total about
one-third of our 1938 exports. The two countries
would, therefore. arnear to be following a parallel
course, if the British are assuring themselves that
the recent reduction does in fact amount to forty percent.
There would seem to be little point in making
further reductions in the quotas, unless we wish to
abolish them altogether.  The balance of the Philippine
quota w1ll permit an export of only 60, 000 tons a month
which will probably not be sufficlent to keep the
four Philippine mines in operation. Any further reduc-
tion would certainly close some if not all of them.
On the other hand, the failure further to restrict
exports will permit Japan to obtain 800,000 tons of
ore from these two souroes during the balance of the
year. This may be presumed to amount, according to the
British estimates attaohed hereto, to about twenty
peroent of the Japanese supnlies of iron during the
remainder of the year. Pig iron from India will account
for an additional six percent. A total embargo on iron
ore

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
ore and pig iron from British and American possessions
might. therefore. result in reducing present Jananese
supplies by about one-fourth. In vlew of the pre-eminent
1mportance of iron in war industry, there would seem to
be very good reasone for taking this step as soon as
a propitious moment occurs.
The effect in the Philionines of a complete embargo
would, of course, be to put the four mines totally out
of business. Some form of relief to the persons engaged
in the industry should certainly be extended by the
United States Government. It is believed that the
character and extent of that relief is a matter upon
which the recommendation of the High Commissioner
should be requested when the time arrives. My belief
1s that the mining companies have already seen the
handwriting on the wall and are taking steps gradually
to liquidate.

Charles W. Yost

NOTE: This metal was sold at Cristobal while in transit; it apparently
is being shipped to New York to pass through that port in bond (in
transit) enroute to Japan. This peculiar routing is not understood
here, nor could we ascertain the reason for it.

Argentine Note Regarding Exohange of Scrap
Iron for Ships.
There 1s no question that the Japanese are anxious
to acquire scrap iron, and it is probable that their
need 1s such that they would be able to send ships to
Argentina to transport the scrap iron to Japan.
2.
The Maritime Commisslon does not belleve that the
Japanese have any ships avallable which they would be
willing to sell or to barter for scrap iron. The shipping
orisis has affected the Japanese as well as other
countries; the Japanese are short of shipping.
PS/LB
94
3.
There is a strong commercial demand in the United
States for scrap 1ron. American concerns are purohasing
serap in Colombia, Cuba, Dominican Republio, Haiti,
Venezuela and El Salvador. There is attaohed a gst gr
recently handed informally to the Dominican Minfitef
In
134

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
In addition to the firms mentioned here, Richard Nathen
and Company of New York have been active 1n 1mporting scrap.
The Nathen firm is made up of naturalized citizens of
German origin, wno, nowever, enjoy a good reputation.
4.
Shipping difficulties are such that it would
probably be very difficult for scrap 1ron, 1f purchased
by American concerns, to be transported to the United States.
The Maritime Commission is quite definite on this point.
5.
The United States Government 1s not now buying or
contemplating buying scrap 1ron.
6.
It would not be feasible in the readily foreseeable
future to make avalleble any 1mportant amount of shipping
tonnage to Argentina.
a) Early in 1939 the Argentines were offered ten
ships of the Swayne & White Line but the
negotiations broke down and the transaction did
not go through. The Argentine Government 1s now
interested 1n acquiring new 8,000 to 12,000 tons
freighters, which of course are not available.
The Argentines are not interested in smaller ships
nor at the present time in second-hand vessels.
Mr. Pardo of the Argentine Embassy has been put
in touch with ship builders and seoond-hand ship
brokere by the Maritime Commission.
b)
In March 1940 the "San Bernardino", a ship owned
by the Pacific Atlantic Steamship Company, of
Portland, Oregon, was sold for $425, 000 to the
Compania de Navigacion "La Interoceania" SA, a
newly-formed Panamanian company, and put under
Panamanian registry and renamed "Atlantioo".
This new Panamanian company was formed early in
1940 by Alberto Dodero, a leading Argentine
shipping man. Dodero stated that he placed the
ship under Panamanian registry because Argentine
law prohibited resale of ships once placed under
Argentine registry.

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.94)
-3-
In August 1940 the ship was in
Montevideo with little prospect of getting
a cargo and Dodero offered the ship to the
Britlsh. The British refused to buy so he
sold 1t to a New York broker for $425, 000.
The New York troker represented Japeneee
intereste end the ship was transferred to
Jepenese registry. Dodero cleimed that he
did not know that the broker was acting for
the Japanese and if he had known he would
not have sold.

Apr1l 27, 1941
Senator Jemes Mead:
Please send me a list of materials exported to Japan
by the United States and the other American Republics at the
present time. Include such items as petroleum and metals.
I suspect that these exiorts are still quite large and since
Japan is a potential enemy of the near future it would pro-
bably be wise to place an embargo on those materials which
are helping to destory Chinese and Japanese lives and which
may be sent back to American soldiers in the form of bombs
and shells.
The wise thing would have been to vlace an embargo when
the Sino-Japanese War started, but it is not too late even
now. It is sneer hypocrisy to send Japan the power to fight
us, and at the same time build a costly fleet to protect our-
seives from our own weapons.
Sincerely yours,
(Signed) Manuel Miletznik

THE AMERICAN REPUBLICS
DEPARTMEN! OF STATE
My dear Mr. Berle:
As of possible interest to you, information has been
received from a reliable confidential source to the effect that the
Japanese are buying up all the lignum vitae that they can obtain in
Nicaragua, and that most of it comes from the Sebaco district.
It is also reported that the Captain of the Guardia in the
Sebaco district forces thc natives to sell lignum vitae at forty
cordobas per ton. It is further renorted that he has men stealing
it for him and that a ton acquired in this manner costs him from
twenty to twenty-five cordobas.
PS/LB
In Managua, it is reported, he sells a ton of lignum vitae
for one hundred and forty cordobas. It is also reported that
President Somoza, of Nicaragua, owns much of the land on which lignum
vitae is found but allows the Captain of the Guardia to obtain the
graft in the manner mentioned above.
It has been reported that possibly President Somoza allows
high Guardia officials to secure funds in the manner noted in order
to keep them in line.
FILED
Sincerely yours,
S

Conclusions.
Gasoline is of primary importance at this time because
of:

aviation grade may continue tc expand because of (1) and (2).
Ample evidence before the officers of tre Tepartment sub-
stantiates the foregoing.
Japan is at present dependent almost entirely upon the
United States for gasoline which can be raised to aviation
grade. The amount of gasoline for which export licenses
have been issued in recent months suggests the possibility,
on the basis of past experience, that Japan is preparing for
a new mejor militery operation. (The possibility exists
also that some of this gesoline mey be reaching Germeny by
way of the U.S.S.R.)
Japanese statistics show gasoline imports of all types
of about 4,300,000 barrels in 1936, wh1ch may perhaps be
considered as a "normal" year. The present arrangements with
producers in the N.E.I. provide for supplies to Japan at
an annuel rate almost equal to Japanese imports of gasoline
from all sources in 1936. Any attempt to reduce total
Japanese gasoline imports of all types to the 1936 level

If it 1s decided that 1mmediate action should be taken

Mr. Welles:
In response to your inquiry (etteched to the cable
from Lima, No. 161 of May 6, regarding Japanese offers of
high prices for all kinds and quantities of ores and
concentrates), I regret that I cannot report that in my
judgment the program for the acquisition of all (or almost
ell) of available supplies of strategic and critical
materials produced in the Latin American countries 1s making
satisfactory headwey. Outside of a few transactions of
importance, it is for the moment in a rether frustrated
state. The Japanese (in many instances aoting for the
Germans) are very aotive in all these markets with bids
markedly higher than the prioes prevailing for these products
before they entered the market. This 1s a condition which
Confidential File
our own program of acquisition must take into account on
ever-increasing scale, and my judgment 1s that it can only
be met by willingness on our part to go a substantial way
to match Japanese price offers. Countries of Latin America
do not feel that as long as we sell commodities like oil
to Japan that we can properly ask them to sacrifice income
to assure the flow of products to the United States rather
than

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
than to Jenan.
They plso of course are not et all edverse
to takinp advantage of the situati n to et t e utmost
possible from tre sele of t e meterinls wuich they hold.
Fortunately, in some situations the chief producers are
Americans and that hae enabled us to handle these situations
vithout havinr to deal vith Japanese competition. It is
the reason why we have been able to put through the largescale
arrangements for the purchases of copper end to keep under
our control large amounts of zinc production in Argentina,
manganese production in Brazil, etc.
These operations carried out ith American companies
end the contract for purchase of Bolivian tin ore make up
almost our whole accomplishment in the field of procurement
up to the present. In brief summary the status of negotiations
of our discussions is as follows:
(1) The comprehensive discussions that have taken

a long list of materials to the United States in conneotion

veraions of existing market prices, will be acceptable to

the Brazilians. But I am doubtful of it. In the case of

However, no actual discussions have yet been undertaken.
So much for a summary review of a few of the most
important situations.
One obstacle has been overcome. Mr. Clayton has been able
to arrange that 1mports that may be brought in by the sub-
sidiary agencies of the RFC for stockpile purposes shall
be brought in as for the use of the Navy, and thereby be not
subject to duty. This puts him in a position in regard to
such metals as tungsten to increase his price offer in
Latin

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
Latin American countries without provoking a rise in prices
inside the United  tates. Mr. Jones end Mr. layton have given
 gooc deal of time and patience t ie onsideration of these
matters. But the fact still remains that there is noone
completely concentrating on them and following them every
hour, studying the statistics of oroduction and of sales to
the Axis Powers, develooing new nitiatives at ever: turn,
and utilizing the services of special buying representatives.
It is my judgment that the program will only be effective
if such concentrated, constant, and aggressive methods are
pursued. The Japs are certainly pursuing them.
Our own prospective needs of all these materiels, plus
the fact that they undoubtedly are imoortant in the war
program of the Axis Powers gives this whole subject greater
importance than ever.
We are in daily conversation with Mr. Clayton and his
staff about one or other of these situations. As an immediate
recommendation I suggest the utility of a conference between
yourself, Mr. Clayton, and Mr. Jones, if he will participate,
with the 1dea of agreeing upon the selection of speoial
representatives - who shall be representatives of the State
Department and of these other agencies - with the sole
assignment

in each of the Latin American countriee T have named.

=== Page 475 === (confidence 0.98)
ADMINISTRATION OF SECTION 6 OF THE ACT ENTITLED,
"AN ACT TO EXPEDITE THE STRENGTHENING OF THE
NATIONAL DEFENSE" APPROVED JULY 2, 1940
A PROCLAMATION
Was eete or the net f onreae entitled
"An Aet to apedite tme strengthenire of the national
defense," approved July 2, l940, provides as follows:
"Whenever the President determines that it is
necessary in the interest of national defense to
prohibit or curtail the exportation of any military
equipment or munitions, or component parts thereof,
or machinery, tools, or material or supplies necessary
for the manufacture, servicing or operation thereof,
he may by proclamation prohibit or curtail such
exportation, except under such rules and regulations
as he shall prescribe. Any such proclanation shall
describe the articles or naterials included in the
prohibition or curtailment contained therein. In
case of the violation of any provision of any procla-
mation, or of eny rule or regulation, issued hereunder,
such violator or violators, upon conviction, shall be
punished by a fine of not more than $10,0o0, or by
imprisonment for not more than two years or by both
such fine and imprisonment. The nuthority granted
in this Act shall terminate June 30, 1942, unless the
Congress shall otherwise provide."
AND WHEREAS by my proclamation no. 2413 of July 2,
1940, ent1t1ed "ADMINISTRATION OF SECTION 6 OF THE ACT
ENTITLED  AN ACT TO EXPEDITE THE STRENGTHENING OF THE
NATIONAL DEFENSE' APPROVED JULY 2, 194O", I procla1med
that upon the recommendation of the Administrator of
Export Control I had determined that it was necessary
in the interest of the national defense that certain
listed articles and materials should not be exported from
the United States except when authorized in each case by
a license as provided for in the said proclamation.
NOW, THEREFORE, I, FRANKLIN D. ROOSEVELT, Pres1dent
of the United States of America, acting under and by virtue
of the authority vested in me by the said act of Congress,
do hereby proclaim that upon the recommendation of the
aforesaid Administrator of Export Control I have determined
that it is necessary in the interest of the national defense
that on and after August 1, 1940, the additional materials
hereinafter listed shall not be exported from the United
States except when authori zed in each case by a license as
provided for in the aforesaid proclamation:
1. Petroleum products
2. Tetraethyl lead
3. Iron and steel scrap

caused the seal of the United Statcs of America to be
affixed.

and of the Independence of the United States of America the

Export
In reference to C O
mor
Dear Sir:
Our business was the collecting of No. 10
tin cans which were discarded by the various state
institutions in New Jersey and New York City.
We took these cans and salvaged or reclaimed the tin
strips from tnem by a secret washing and cutting
process. we began experimenting with the process in
1933 and finally equipped the plant to manufacture the
same. At one time, we held certain orders from Japan
for the finisred product.
About the time we had the business properly
organized and running smoothly, certain embargoes were
placed upon the export, thereof. As it was practically
impossible to sell the same domestically at a profit,
we simply closed the plant down after operating it for
about two or three years. At the time of closing, we
were turning out approximately 1oo tons per month of
tin strips 6" x 18".
After working about three years and investing
considerable capital, we were forced over-night by
adverse legislation to take our loss and like it. At
the present time we are not in the tin business and
under no circumstances would we re-enter the industry
or assume any financial responsibility.
If, rowever, it becomes imperative that our
Nation begin the reclaimation of tin strips from cast
off cans, we shall be more than willing to impart our
knowledge to your representative without any kind of
remuneration whatsoever. I personally spent four
MAY2-1
years in experimenting with the process and for your
FILED
information, I desire to state that at least 20,000
tons of empty cans were wasted each month alone in
New York City and New Jersey. It is a wide field that
1941
no one has any knowledge of whatsoever excepting myself
and one other individual.

=== Page 498 === (confidence 0.94)
Please don't gather from this that I am
attempting to obtain something from it economically.
Even though my own government practically ruined me
financially, I am willing to give you the benefit of
all I know about it gratis.
Respectfully yours,
JAoeteluic
FRED D. ROCKHILL
FDR HS

=== Page 503 === (confidence 0.99)
Japanese Purchases of Manganese
Ore in Chile
Copies of correspondence on the above sulject
have recently been received from His Majesty's Commercial
Counsellor at Santiago, Chile. The upshot of this correspondence
is that the Corporacion del Fomento, which is a governmental
institution designed to aid local industries in Chile, has or-
ganised a special department to handle the purchase from
small miners and resale to the Japanese of Chilean manganese
ores. A mining engineer by the name of Enrique Vial is in
charge of this department. It is known already to have con-
tracted with the Japanese for the sale of 5,000 tons, though
it has as yet secured only3,500 tons.
The minimum grade of ore accepted by the Corporacion
1s 42% and for this the Corporacion pays $380 (pesos) but off this
price it deducts expenses for sampling, weighing etc. amounting
to something like #60 (pesos) per ton. Our information is that
for the small miner who cannot deliver ore of minimum grade of
46% which is the requirement of Messrs. Grace & Co. who buy for
shipment to the U.8., the Corporacion's offer is highly satis-
factory. The Corporacion is, furthermore, said to be trying to
arrange that a special manganese dollar be fixed in order that

=== Page 504 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
they may have a larger margin between what they receive
in U.S. currency from the Japanese and what they pay the
small miners in Chile. This arrangement may be possible
since Japanese-Chilean payments are cleared through a
Japenese-United States dollar account and presumably the
Corporacion del Fomento can arrange with the Chilean Exchange
Control to give them a special rate for these Japanese
transactions.
29th March.4941
RFB8DM/vm

states to see taat eny laws  ed ty tr ure t t nut ual tovan-

when such laws are framed that there are no loopholes.
I refer specifically to the embarro, Executive Order No. 8669 of
Februaiy 1, 1941, which w:s placed on the exportetion of steel drums
55 gallon content and under, fille: vith asoline and oil products.
This embarro was made so that these products woula not be exported
to Japan and any of the Axis powers. However, the loophole in this
law was great enou h to permit tie exportation by the thousands of
wcoden barrels 'i: led witn oils and . asoline. I personally know of
thous nds of such barrels wnie. are bein: exp orted to Japan through
New Orleans nd other Julf Coast ports. These exports have already
defeated the purpose of the embargo.
A steel tarrel eighs approxinetely 40 lbs. of scrap steel, whereas
the six or eight hoops of scrap sterl on a wooden barrel weigh a total
of fron 20 to 25 lbs. Tnis is not conserving ste 1 to any extent, if
that was the reason for the embargo.
Furthermore the danger to ships carrying these barrels is very great.
Wooden barrels can be dropped from Airplanes in very much the same way
as a bomb and can be used for incendiary purposes. The trafficking of
these barrels has been more or less of an experirent b. shippers to see
if they pass throuh the customs unhampered. So far they have been
successful in passing tihrou, h every consignment. To prove this matter
a little further, I have enclosed a clipping taken from today's "P. M."
and I have outlined in red pencil the shipr.ent wich shows 5,200 wood
barrels, containing approximately 55 gallons of oil per barrel.
The embargo should either include wooden barrels in its text, or it
should be lifted to permit the exportation of steel barrels.

In the course of a conversation which I had this
morning with Mr. Walden, of the Standard-Vacuum Oil
Company, Mr. Walden made to me three interesting statements
which have a bearing on the above. (1) He confirmed the
information that the anount of oil sent by the Anglo-Iranian
Company in 1940 to Japan under the contract had been small.
(2) He said that the British Government could absolutely
control exports of oil fron the whole Persian Gulf area,
as they control the Anglo-Iranian Company and as California
Texas is a "British company, Nassau registered." (3) He
said that he had not the slightest doubt but that the British
Government would withhold Persian Gulf oil from Japan
completely if the United States would, by agreement, shut
down on exports of oil to Japan from this country.
PA/H:SKH:ZMK

=== Page 539 === (confidence 0.96)
How
G mThs. Bit. 
I.20
March 19th, 1941
Qil for Jaren
On March 3rd the Ambassador discussed the question of oil
for Japan with the Secretary of State, and the sale by the Anglo-
Iranian Oil Company of oil to the Japanese was mentioned. In order
to place this transaction in proper perspective, the attached table
has been prepared to show total oil imports into Japan and Manchukuo
for the years 1937 to 1940 inclusive, from all sources.
2.
First shipments from Iran occurred in 1940 and amount to
O.% of Japanese imports for that year. These sales were part of a
contract to supply 12 cargoes, or approximately 1,125,000 barrels,
of crude oil f.o.b. Abadan. To date only 278,000 barrels have been
lifted and, the contract expiring on June 30th, 1941, it is unlikely
that further quantities will be taken because the Japanese are finding
it difficult to provide vessels.
3.
The circumstances giving rise to this contract may be
briefly mentioned. The Anglo-Iranian suffered a considerable re-
duction in the export markets as a result of the war. This placed
them in a difficult position with the Shah, whose constant demand
for increased royalties was fully exploited by the Japanese, and
the Anglo-Iranian has been forced to pay large advance royalties
to the Iranian Government to avoid loss of their concession. It
would have been difficult at the time the contract was made to
justify, not only to the Shah but also to the Japanese, a refusal
to sell & relatively small quantity of crude oil which the Japanese
were required to lift in their own vessels and when they were and
are free to buy not only crude oil but also the more valuable manu-
factured products of oil from alternative sources.
PH:MS
British Embassy,
Washington, D.C.

I understand that a firn offer hus been made by certain
copper mines in Argentina for shipment to Japan of as much tonnage
as can be supplied at this time, on the basis of proper content, at
the New York price.
It has also come to my attention that considerable quanti-
ties of the glycerine produced in Argentina by large soap works
(which as you know is used in making dynamite) are being sold to local
Japanese buyers in Buenos Aires for from 25% or even 30% more than can
be procured for this commodity in New York. It is understood in Buenos
Aires that this product is absolutely indispensable for Japan. Loyal
American citizens in Buenos Aires recommend that the United States take
steps to prevent so much of this commodity from falling into Japanese
hands.
If I may ofter a personal suggestion, might it not be
advisable for the State Department to cable the United States Embassy
81
in Buenos Aires, to ascertain exactly what could be done in this matter?
No one knows better than your good self how important this material is
strategically.

8
know why such faint-hearted diplomacy is followed?
6
4

12

4

playing continously tricks upon the Government of the United
States to evade all the proclamations and laws,just to make a
profit from the murder of innocent people.
6
2

Here,we are spending billions of dollars of peoples
money for defense,but at the same time feeding the enemies with
6

ter,but of course, the great diplomats of our State Department

not only from the Asbestos Corporation of Canada, but
also from American or South African companies, provided
that its annual quota is not exceeded.

Christian Science Monitor
U. S. Weighing
Japan Oil Ban,
Axis Fund Tieup
RECEIVED
By a Staff Correspondent of
The Christian Science Momitor
WASHINGTON, May 2-The
United States may soon place an
airtight embargo on the shipment.
of oil and gasoline to Japan and
also "freeze" the American-held
funds of all foreign nations.
thereby permitting closer super-
Ass:stant
vision over Axis expenditures
of State
here.
For the first time today, Secre-
MAY 1 5 1941
tary of State Cordell Hull admit-
ted that the "freezing" was under
consideration and he drew a com-
parison between that question and
MR. AHESON
the one raised by the continued
shipments of American petroleum
894.24
products to Japan. The Secretary's
action in linking the two questions
was taken as an indication that
the long-delayed action against
Japan might be near at hand.
The importance of the Secre-
tary's hint concerning Japan is
underlined by the fact that previ-
ously he had carefully avoided
..S
answering all questions regarding
the continued shipment of Ameri-
can oil and gasoline to Japan. That
the Secretary was willing to refer
Daportment of Sh
to the petroleum problem is taken
to mean that it has been given
much study and that a final de-
cision may be near.
Although Secretary Hull said
that no decision on the "freezing"
had yet been reached, there are
substantial reports that action will
2
be taken shortly.
SION
If the Government decides to
"freeze" the funds of all nations
here, it would enable Treasury
Department experts to prevent the
Axis from using its large reserves
in this country in furtherance of
its war against Britain. Under
such procedure the Treasury would
dole out to Germans, Italians and
probably Japanese officials here
only such funds as it knew would
not be used in their present cam-
paigns of aggression.
On the other hand, such a "freez
ing" would not injure Britain nor
any other nation having Amer-
ican sympathy, since the Treasury
could release as much of their
funds as it sees it.
Until now the State Department

move on the grounds that it would
destroy its diplematie n

Prese Conference. At the Waite  re corference tats morning. Presi-
dent Roosevelt read a letter whien mo informed the correspondents he had written
to Director General Knudsen and Associate Director Gen  ral Hillman of the OPM
(see below), emphasizing the urgent neccssity of expanding and speeding up the
manufacture and use of criticrl machine tocls. Asked if he could give the details
of his letter to Mr. Knudeen nd Mr. Hilimn the President renlied that e canvass
was being made t rinill d w r  erine frem wh ict they had been

skilled workers who heve not been "me ri i" to m chines for the defense effort.
He said thot the Wer Department at pr  t wos making a recheck and a new study
in order that we would not turn into infntrymen or ertillerymen various young
men under 35 years of pge who ere purfictly capable of running mechines. He said
that it was necessary to maintain a constant flow of young men into the necessary
defense professions that must be kopt going. with regard to whnt professions were
essential for defense he said he had reached the conclusion that doctors and
engineers, for instence, were eseential and thet lawycrs like himself end news-
papermen were not. A correspondent inquired whether the twcnty-four hour use of
mechine tools would result in longor hours for workers. The President said thet
he believed thet this would not be so. A correspondent remerked thet the National
Association of Monufacturers yesterday announced thet it hed turned over to
Mr. Knudsen a list of medium sized plants in the United States, some of which
employed only five men, and he inquired how far thc President's program as out-
lined in this letter would go. The Prisident replied that his program would go
oven down to the smallest plant. He said that what they were trying to do was to
effect a pooling of all the useful machines in the country end to see thet they
are used effectively, where they are, or if they are not, to move them where they
can be used. Asked if this program contemplated the release of mon already taken
into the Army to civilian production duties, the President replied that this was
pert of the present study.
A correspondent inquired if new plans were being considered to speed the out-
put of airplanes by automobile fectories end he remarked thet there were reports
that automobile plants would produce 500 4-cngine bombers monthly on an ac
celerated program. The President replied that he could not give figures and that
there has been under way for several months the production of airplanes or air-
plane' parts by automobile manufacturers.
A correspondent inquired if there were any new developmonts concerning the
Coast Guard. The President replied in the negative.
A correspondent rcferred to press reports from London published today stating
thet the British Government was attempting to bring the United States into line
with its own policy for an economic blockade of Germany and for the general frees-
ing of Axis funds and he inquired if the President could comment. The President
referred the inquirer to the State Department.
Asked if he had any position in mind for Myron Taylor (who served recently
as special representative of the President to the Vetican) the President romarked
that he understood thet Mr. Taylor had leased a house in Washington. Ho added
that the state of Mr. Taylor's health did not permit him to accept a position at
this time.
A correspondent referred to a press despatch fron Borlin by John Cudahy
(former Ambassador to Belgium and Polend) appearing in the press today and he in-
quired if the President had seen the despatch. (In the despatch Mr. Cudahy
quoted Dr. Goebbels as stating thet the German General Staff hed made an estimate
from every military angle, strategically and tactically, of German troop landimge
on American soil end had reached the conclusion that an invasion in sufficient
numbers to occupy and hold any territory of the Western Hemisphere was impossible.
He quoted Dr. Goebbels as adding thet the Gorman General Staff was equally
convinoed

Critical Machine Tools. The follo in is the text of a letter dated April 30
sent by tho President to Director Generol Knudsen end Associete Director General
Hillman of thc OPM:
"My recent discussions with you hove emphasized in my mind the urgent neces-
sity of expanding and speeding up the monufacture and usc of critical machine
tobls. I have watchcd the stceiy and substantial growth of the machine tool
industry during the past months. At the same time I have seen the critical ma-
chines in our defense plants used in an ever growing number of hours cach week. I
know that this increase has been caused by the herd work of yourselves, of your
associetes and of the men who marng and work in the plants throughout the nation.
"But it is not enough. The ever increasing demends for munitions, planes
end ships, caused by the criticel situation which confronts our nation, requires
thet they be produced in even larger quantities and ahrad of the schedules as-
signed to them. It is essential thet industry continue to incrcase the number of
vitel mechines manufactured nnd that every single criticel machine in the United
States be used the meximum number of hours each weck.
"Every effort should be mede to utilize to the very limit those critical ma-
chines; if they be in defense plents by increased hours of operntion on the work
at hend; if in other plants by finding defense items or parts for them to make or.
as a last resort, by moving the tools to defensc plants where they may be urgently
needed.
"Our problem is to see to it that there is no idle criticel machine in the
United Stetes. The goal should be to work thcse mechines twenty four hours a day
and seven days a week, relieving the mechines only for such time as is required
for overheuling and repair.
"The country should be further combed for men who have hed experience on
these machines. We should ask them to transfer their efforts to this operation
which is so essential to our defense. No effort or justifiable expense should be
spared in speeding this program, in order to obtain the objective which our
national interests require.
"Workers and managers will, I believe, join with you with spirit and deter-
mination in pursuing and achieving this goal et the earliest possible moment."

Americans in Athens. The Dopartment todey informed the press that a telegram
has been received from the American Minister et Athens, dated April 29, transmitted
via the German Foreign Office in Berlin, stating that all Americans in Athens are
safe and well. The telegram adds thet the occupation of the city has been carried
out in an orderly and peaceable manner.

May Day Parade. In the face of denunciations by labor unions and labor lead-
ers, who termed the event entircly Communistic, the May Day parade was held in New
York yesterday. The press stated thet it received little attention and that
police using counting ma chines recorded 18, 788 marchers, despite the claims of the
United Mey Day Committee that it numbered 200, O00. The police stated that 24,000
marchers were counted entering Union Square, scene of a program by the Committee.
and that many of the paraders hed hurried beck along the route and passed the
entrance more than once.
National Defense Sevings Program. The press reported that Treasury officials.
on the basis of first dey sales, predicted overwhelming success for the national
Defense Savings Stamp and. Bond sale.
Finland. The Treasury Department announced thet Minister Procope of Finland
and Secretery Morgenthau yesterday executed an agreement under which the Republic
of Finland will undertake to pay $235, 398 to the United States, in ten annual pay-
ments with interest at three percent. The ebove sun was payable by Finland to the
United States on December 15, 1940 but was postponed under a joint resolution of
Congress approved on June 15, 1940. Under the terns of the agreement. Finland
has agreed to pay anmuelly $27, 390.12. These payments are to be made in two in-
tallments of $13, 695.06 on June 15 and on December 15. The first payment under
this agreement will be due on June 15, 1941.
Stook Market. Volume of trading yesterday: 310,000 shares. Dow-Jones
closing stock averages: Industrials--115.30; Railroeds--28.33; Utilities--17.84.
Stocks were irregular today in quiet trading. The utility averege was at a
new low since Sept ember 27, 1938. Curb stocks were irregular ani Chicago stocks
were nixed. In Chicago wheat closed up to half a cent higher and corn closed
fraotionally lower.

necessary in the interest of national defense to prohibit
or curtail the exportation of any military equipment or
munitions, or component parts thereof, or machinery, tool
or material, or supplies necessary for the manufacture,
servicing, or operation thereof, he may by proolamation
prohibit

May PO, 1941
Senator Aurthur Vandenberg, Senate, Wasnington, D. C.
Senator Prentiss Brown, Senate, Wasnington, D. C.
William W. Blackney, House of Representatives, Washington, D. C.
Gentlemen:
I note by tne Congressional Record in a speech
recently made that this Country is still shipping more than
a million barrels of oil monthly to Japan, to say nothing of
the other materials and supulies.
On one hand we are extending credits and supplies
to China by R.F.C. and otherwise in order to give them the aid
to defend themselves in the war of aggression of Japan, and on
the other hand we are delivering to Japan oil and other supplies
to feed their war machines in their aggression upon China.
What is wrong with our Department of State and
Congress in permitting the shipment of supulies which can be
used for war purposes to the aggressor nations?
Very truly yours,
(Signed)
1b
Jack J. Lande

=== Page 622 === (confidence 0.98)
Hen stead, Texas,
May 1, 141
Honorable Tom Connally
Washinrton, D. C.
Dear Sir:
I am writing to ask why are we furnishing Japan with
petroleum. According to figures of the Bureau of
foreign and Domestic Commerce our exports of petroleum
products to Japan in tne month of March of this year
was 817733 barrels.
It is very strange that we should be builaing an army
and making all kinds of preparations to defend our-
selves against Japan and Germany and still furnish them
with things so necessary to carry on a war. We are
furnishing China and England supplies to defend them-
selves with against tnem anu are expecting any day to
nave to defend ourselves against tnem both and they
are gathering al? they can so as to have a reserve to
use against us. I am on the draft board here and I
tell you it looks hard to draft a lot of young men to
go and be butcnered by people that we are furnishing
the material to do it with. You are one of our law-
makers and we are looking to you to see that nothing
like that goes on hoping that you will see that it is
stopped.
Yours truly,
(Signed) W. W. Chapman

THE AMERICAN LEGION
ffice of
Department Servi
Veterans A iniatration
Waco, Texas
May 10, 1941.
Hon. Tom Connally
Member of Conress
Washingtn, D. c.
Dear Senator Coi ally:
As an ex-Marine of the World War, a member
of the American Legion and a true citizen of America, I was
greatly shocked upon reading in the Waco Record, Friday, May 16,
1941, that 14,O00,000 barrels of gasol1ne 1s being shipped each
day from our shores to our potential eneny, Japan. Japan is
an acknowledged member of the Axis and unfriendly to our
principals and standard of life, a country with whom we may soon
be engaged in mortal combat to ireserve our rights and liberties.
To aid in strengthening ner notential power and to thus assist
ner in ner attempted destruction of a sister Republic, is
UNAMERICAN to say the least.
As the Waco Record puts it: "whoever in
this nation 1s responsible for the shipment of 14,000,C00
barrels of gasoline each day to Japan is America's No. 1
Fifth Columnist".
Mr. Connally, as a Senator from this
liberty loving State of Texas, I implore and trust that your
voice and strength will be quickly raised to stop this Un-
american activity.
Sincerely,
(Signed)
H. V. ROYSTON
ASST. DEPARTMENT SERVICE OFFICER
THE AMERICAN LEGION.

=== Page 648 === (confidence 0.98)
Rev. Benjamin S. Winchester
Currituck Road
Newton, Sonnectieut
Hon. Francis Maloney,
U. S. Senate,
Washington, D. C.
y dear Senator:
A Titilerour meete pere oceuclon y t consider
current phases o: international relations. The enclosed
statement has come into our hands. I am asked to write
you to inquire whether the statement is a true picture
of the facts; and, if so, what justification, if any,
there 1s for such proceedure. Aside from the question
as to the prcpriety, under any circumstances, of continu-
ing to su:ply oil to Japan, we nave noted that it has
become necessary, for lack of tankers, to ship oil from
Texas for domestic use by rail, a much more expensive
method. It would seem to be a wise policy for us to
keep our oil at home, rather than ship it to Japan.
Our little group would greatly appreciate a clear state-
ment and explanation of the situation.
Very truly yours,
(Signed) B. S. Winchester
